=== NOTES 4:83 | India – Horticulture, Plantation Crops, Livestock, Fisheries and Agro-based Activities
## SUB 0 | Sericulture, Lac and Apiculture
[83:0:0] # India's silks
[83:0:1] TABLE head: Silk | Main State(s) | Food plant / note
  [83:0:1.0] Mulberry (about 90% of output) | Karnataka (largest), Andhra Pradesh (2nd), TN, WB | Mulberry
  [83:0:1.1] Tropical tasar (kosa) | Jharkhand (largest), Chhattisgarh, Odisha, AP, Telangana | Terminalia (Arjun, Nallamaddi), Sal
  [83:0:1.2] Oak (temperate) tasar | Manipur, Uttarakhand, HP, J&K | Oak; China is the major world producer
  [83:0:1.3] Eri (endi, "peace silk") | Assam, Meghalaya, other NE States | Castor, tapioca
  [83:0:1.4] Muga (golden silk) | Assam only (65.6%), Meghalaya (16.67%) | Som and Soalu; *Antheraea assamensis*; about 1% of India's silk
[83:0:2] - India is the **only country producing all five** commercial silks (mulberry, tropical tasar, oak tasar, eri, muga) – older questions say "all four types".
[83:0:3] - World: **China 1st**, **India 2nd** in raw silk (FAO 2023).
[83:0:4] - **Karnataka** = largest silk State: 44.15% of mulberry and 41.69% of raw silk (2023-24); also largest for silk yarn and silk textiles.
[83:0:5] - **Karnataka + AP** once gave more than **three-fourths** of raw silk; now about 59% (2023-24).
[83:0:6] - Statement trap: AP is **not** the largest mulberry producer (2nd); **Meghalaya is not** the largest Eri producer (Assam is).
[83:0:7] - **Central Silk Board** – **Bengaluru**. AP tasar: East Godavari and ASR (Rampachodavaram).
[83:0:8] # Not from the silkworm
[83:0:9] - **Gossamer silk** = spider silk. **Ahimsa** silk = peace silk (moth emerges first).
[83:0:10] - **Sabra silk** = "cactus silk", a plant fibre from agave/aloe leaves – **not** from a silkworm (Eri, Muga, Kosa are silkworm silks).
[83:0:11] # Lac and bees
[83:0:12] TABLE head: Activity | Product
  [83:0:12.0] Sericulture | Silk
  [83:0:12.1] Apiculture | Honey and bee-wax (egg → larva → pupa → adult)
  [83:0:12.2] Lac culture | Lac – the resin secreted by the lac insect → shellac, sealing wax
  [83:0:12.3] Pisciculture / Floriculture | Fish / flowers
[83:0:13] - **Jharkhand** = largest lac producer today (Chhattisgarh next); older books and keys say **Bihar** (Jharkhand was part of Bihar till 2000) – e.g. "largest sealing wax State = Bihar".
[83:0:14] > Exam angle: Karnataka (mulberry, raw silk, yarn), Jharkhand (tropical tasar, lac), Manipur (oak tasar), Assam (muga, eri); India 2nd after China.
[83:0:15] ~~ Sources: PMF Human & Economic Geography 05 p.52-53 (silk types, Muga unique to Assam, India the only country with all five); app notes 4:83 (sub 6); Sabra silk, Jharkhand lac: general knowledge, not checked
## SUB 1 | Livestock – Breeds and Animal Husbandry
[83:1:0] # Indian cattle and buffalo breeds
[83:1:1] TABLE head: Breed | Species / use | Native region
  [83:1:1.0] Sahiwal | Cattle – best milch indigenous breed | Punjab (Montgomery, now Pakistan)
  [83:1:1.1] Gir / Kankrej | Cattle (milch / dual) | Gujarat
  [83:1:1.2] Tharparkar | Cattle (dual) | Western border region of Rajasthan (Jaisalmer, Barmer, Jodhpur), Kutch
  [83:1:1.3] Rathi, Nagori | Cattle | Rajasthan
  [83:1:1.4] Ongole | Cattle (draught, dual) | Prakasam, AP
  [83:1:1.5] Punganur | Dwarf (small-size) cattle | Chittoor, AP
  [83:1:1.6] Hallikar, Deoni, Malvi | Draught cattle | Karnataka, Maharashtra-Karnataka, MP
  [83:1:1.7] Murrah, Bhadawari | Buffalo | Haryana, UP-MP
  [83:1:1.8] Jamunapari, Barbari | Goat | UP
  [83:1:1.9] Asil (Aseel), Kadaknath | Poultry (Kadaknath = black meat, MP) | AP / MP
  [83:1:1.10] Ghungroo | Pig | West Bengal
[83:1:2] - Match asked: **Asil – chicken, Catla – fish, Ghungroo – pig, Rathi – cattle**.
[83:1:3] - Frieswal: synthetic cattle (HF × Sahiwal), 7,000 kg milk per lactation.
[83:1:4] # Exotic breeds
[83:1:5] - **Holstein-Friesian** (Netherlands) = **highest milk yield** in the world; Brown Swiss, Jersey, Dutch Belted, Blaarkop give less.
[83:1:6] - **Jersey** = used to cross-breed **non-descript cattle in poor feed and fodder areas** (small, hardy, high fat); HF needs good feed.
[83:1:7] # Animal facts
[83:1:8] - Milk fat: **buffalo** about 7% > goat, cow (about 4%) > camel.
[83:1:9] - Gestation: **goat about 150 days** (shortest) < cow about 280 days < donkey about 12 months < camel about 13 months.
[83:1:10] - Hot (arid, semi-arid) zones: sheep breed mainly in the monsoon → main **lambing season = winter**.
[83:1:11] - Scientific names: *Capra hircus* = **goat**; *Ovis aries* = sheep; *Bos taurus/indicus* = cattle.
[83:1:12] - Fodder: ***Trifolium alexandrinum* = Berseem** (Egyptian clover, rabi legume); Lucerne = *Medicago sativa*.
[83:1:13] - World wool: **China** is the largest producer (overtook **Australia**, the older answer, in the mid-2010s).
[83:1:14] > Exam angle: Sahiwal (milch), Tharparkar (Rajasthan border), Holstein (yield), Jersey (low-input cross-breeding), Punganur (dwarf).
[83:1:15] ~~ Sources: PMF Human & Economic Geography 06 p.28 (Holstein and Jersey as high-yield dairy breeds); AP Socio-Economic Survey 2024-25 p.69 (Ongole, Punganur, Godavari buffalo, Nellore sheep, Aseel); app notes 4:83 (sub 3); web search summary (FAOSTAT-based reports: China largest wool producer); gestation, Jersey use, lambing: general knowledge, not checked
## SUB 2 | Dairying and the White Revolution
[83:2:0] # Milk
[83:2:1] - India = **world's largest milk producer**: **247.87 MT** (2024-25), per capita **485 g/day**.
[83:2:2] - India also leads in **butter and ghee** (milk-based), not meat, pork or cheese.
[83:2:3] - Top milk States 2024-25: **Uttar Pradesh** (15.66%), Rajasthan (14.82%).
[83:2:4] # White Revolution
[83:2:5] - **Operation Flood** (**1970**, by **NDDB**) = milk production and distribution through cooperatives (Anand pattern).
[83:2:6] - **Dr Verghese Kurien** = **Father of the White Revolution** (not P.J. Kurien, Swaminathan).
[83:2:7] - **NDDB** – **Anand** (Gujarat); **NDRI** – **Karnal** (Haryana).
[83:2:8] # Schemes
[83:2:9] - **Rashtriya Gokul Mission** (Dec 2014): development and conservation of **indigenous bovine breeds**; set up **Gokul Grams** and **National Kamdhenu Breeding Centres** (see row 84).
[83:2:10] - Animal Husbandry Infrastructure Development Fund: milk- and meat-processing capacity.
[83:2:11] - **20th Livestock Census (2019)**: 535.78 million livestock, 851.81 million poultry; **21st** (2024-25) fully digital.
[83:2:12] - Livestock and fisheries grew faster than crops (fisheries about 8.8%, livestock about 7.1% a year, FY15-FY24).
[83:2:13] > Exam angle: Operation Flood → NDDB → Anand → Kurien; NDRI is at Karnal, not Anand.
[83:2:14] ~~ Sources: Rocket Indian Geography p.53 (White Revolution = milk) and p.68 (NDRI Karnal, NDDB Anand); app notes 4:83 (sub 4); PMF Human & Economic Geography 06 p.32 (Operation Flood cooperatives)
## SUB 3 | Horticulture – Fruits, Propagation and States
[83:3:0] # India's rank and States
[83:3:1] - India = **2nd in fruits and vegetables** (China 1st); India's share of world fruit about 13.6% (2014) → key "15%".
[83:3:2] - Horticulture 362.08 MT (2024-25), above foodgrains; about 33% of agricultural GVA.
[83:3:3] TABLE head: Produce | Leading State / place
  [83:3:3.0] Mango | Uttar Pradesh largest (AP 2nd); first mango Agri Export Zone in UP (2001-02)
  [83:3:3.1] Orange | Nagpur (Maharashtra)
  [83:3:3.2] Banana | Tamil Nadu / Andhra Pradesh (not Maharashtra)
  [83:3:3.3] Potato | Uttar Pradesh
  [83:3:3.4] Litchi | Bihar
  [83:3:3.5] Pineapple | Meghalaya, Tripura
  [83:3:3.6] Kiwi | Arunachal Pradesh (over 50%); also the "Orchid State"
  [83:3:3.7] All fruits | Andhra Pradesh largest fruit State (about 15.6%); 1st in cocoa productivity
  [83:3:3.8] Loose flowers | Tamil Nadu now (Karnataka in older keys)
  [83:3:3.9] Lakadong high-curcumin turmeric | Meghalaya (not Tripura)
[83:3:4] - APPSC: fruit **at risk because of a weak genetic base = banana** (Cavendish, vegetatively propagated; Panama wilt).
[83:3:5] # Mango varieties
[83:3:6] - **Amrapali** = **Dasheri × Neelam** (IARI Pusa, 1971); Mallika = Neelam × Dasheri.
[83:3:7] - Dasheri, Langra, Chausa are **alternate (irregular) bearers**; regular bearers: Neelam, Totapuri, Amrapali, Dasheri-51.
[83:3:8] # Propagation
[83:3:9] - Grafting: rooted plant = **stock**; shoot from donor = **scion**.
[83:3:10] - **Sweet orange – grafting/budding**; **jasmine – layering**.
[83:3:11] # Plants asked
[83:3:12] - Pineapple = ***Ananas comosus*** (Bromeliaceae).
[83:3:13] - Portuguese brought cashew, tomato, chilli, potato, tobacco, papaya, pineapple, guava.
[83:3:14] - **Sea buckthorn**: cold-desert shrub of high altitudes; **checks soil erosion and desertification**, **nutritional value** (vitamin C); not a biodiesel source, timber of no value.
[83:3:15] > Exam angle: India 2nd in fruits; UP mango; Nagpur orange; Amrapali parents; scion vs stock.
[83:3:16] ~~ Sources: app notes 4:83 (sub 0, sub 1); mango export zone, bearing habit, propagation, sea buckthorn: general knowledge, not checked
## SUB 4 | World Rank – Milk, Fruits, Silk, Fish
[83:4:0] TABLE head: Rank | Commodity
  [83:4:0.0] 1st | Milk, butter and ghee, dry onions; rice (overtook China in 2024-25)
  [83:4:0.1] 2nd | Fruits, vegetables (China 1st), potatoes, wheat, sugarcane, eggs, raw silk (China 1st), fish and aquaculture (China 1st)
[83:4:1] - "India is the biggest producer of vegetables" – **wrong** (2nd).
[83:4:2] - Before 2024-25, "India is the second largest producer of rice" was true; now India is **1st**.
[83:4:3] - India = among the largest producers of sugarcane (2nd after Brazil).
[83:4:4] - Eggs 2024-25: **149.11 billion** (India 2nd); horticulture 362.08 MT.
[83:4:5] > Exam angle: 1st = milk; 2nd = fruits, vegetables, silk, fish, aquaculture.
[83:4:6] ~~ Sources: app notes 4:83 (sub 0); PMF Environment 07 p.37 (India overtook China as largest rice producer in 2024-25, 28% of world output); PMF Human & Economic Geography 06 p.25 (India 2nd in rice, older edition)
## SUB 5 | Fisheries and the Blue Revolution
[83:5:0] TABLE head: Type | Meaning
  [83:5:0.0] Inland | Raising and capturing fish in fresh water (rivers, tanks, reservoirs) and brackish water
  [83:5:0.1] Marine | Coastal and deep-sea fishing
  [83:5:0.2] Capture / culture | Capture = harvest of wild stock / culture = controlled farming (aquaculture)
  [83:5:0.3] Cold-water | 5-25°C; trout above 1,500 m
  [83:5:0.4] Mariculture | Farming in sea water – cultured pearls (pearl oysters), seaweed, mussels
  [83:5:0.5] Pisciculture | Fish farming (fish raised in tanks, enclosures)
[83:5:1] - **Blue Revolution** = **fish / pisciculture** – not poultry, milk or oilseeds.
[83:5:2] - India = **2nd** in fish and in **aquaculture** (China 1st); inland gives over 75% of India's fish.
[83:5:3] - **West Bengal** was the leading fish producer when asked (A true); about 90% of its fish is **inland**, not coastal (R false). **Now Andhra Pradesh** leads (about 30% of India's fish).
[83:5:4] - Hilsa (Bengal's favourite) is reported to take about **3 years** to reach 1 kg.
[83:5:5] - Institutes: CMFRI, CIFT – Kochi; CIFA – Bhubaneswar. PMMSY – 2020.
[83:5:6] > Exam angle: inland = fresh water; Blue = fish; WB (old) vs AP (now) as top producer.
[83:5:7] ~~ Sources: Rocket Indian Geography p.53 (Blue Revolution = fish/fisheries/aquaculture) and p.69 (CIFT, CIFA, CMFRI); AP Socio-Economic Survey 2024-25 p.74 (AP about 30% of national fish); app notes 4:83 (sub 5); mariculture, Hilsa: general knowledge, not checked

  [83:7:0.1] White | Milk
  [83:7:0.2] Blue | Fish (not poultry)

## SUB 8 | Plantation Crops
[83:8:0] - Main plantation crops: **tea, rubber, coconut, coffee** (not sunflower, soyabean, grapes).
[83:8:1] - **Ministry of Commerce**: tea, coffee, rubber (and spices); **Ministry of Agriculture**: coconut, arecanut, oil palm, cocoa, cashew.
[83:8:2] - **Rubber**: **Kerala about 90%** of India's output (APPSC 2003: "more than 90%"); then TN, Karnataka.
[83:8:3] - **Cashew**: Maharashtra is now the largest producer; older books and keys name **Kerala** (largest producer and processor in earlier decades).
[83:8:4] - **Coffee**: Karnataka largest; the edible coffee "bean" is keyed as the **perisperm** (APPSC 2010), though botanists describe it as mainly **endosperm**.
[83:8:5] - Assam – largest tea producer. Coconut – Kerala, Karnataka, TN, AP. **Arecanut** – plantation crop of **tropical humid** climate, Karnataka, Kerala. Cashew – a growth-engine crop of AP.
[83:8:6] > Exam angle: the four plantation crops; Kerala = rubber; Commerce vs Agriculture ministries.
[83:8:7] ~~ Sources: PMF Human & Economic Geography 05 p.79 (Kerala 90% of rubber); app notes 4:83 (sub 2); web search summary (coffee seed anatomy, endosperm with perisperm remnant as silver skin); cashew: general knowledge, not checked
## SUB 9 | Agro-processing
[83:9:0] - Processed food about **23%** of agri-food exports (2024-25); **APEDA Act 1985**.
[83:9:1] TABLE head: Scheme | Year | Feature
  [83:9:1.0] PM Kisan SAMPADA | 2017 | Central Sector; integrated cold chain
  [83:9:1.1] Mega Food Parks | From 2008 | AP: 2 operational – Srini (Chittoor), Godavari Mega Aqua (West Godavari)
  [83:9:1.2] PMFME | 2020 | Aatmanirbhar Bharat; One District One Product
[83:9:2] - AP sugar mills: Godavari districts, Visakhapatnam, Chittoor.
[83:9:3] ~~ Sources: app notes 4:83 (sub 8)

=== OWN MCQs of 4:83 (78)
[nfc8ff7f80af] sub0
  Which State is listed as the second-largest producer of raw silk in India?
  West Bengal | *Andhra Pradesh | Telangana | Tamil Nadu
  x: Andhra Pradesh is listed as the second-largest producer of raw silk, after Karnataka.
[nefd1def55f7] sub0
  Which silk variety is endemic to Assam and is produced by the silkworm Antheraea assamensis?
  Tasar silk | Mulberry silk | *Muga silk | Eri silk
  x: Muga silk is endemic to Assam and is produced by Antheraea assamensis.
[nea81a297902] sub0
  Which silk–food plant pair is correctly matched?
  Mulberry — Castor and tapioca | *Eri — Castor and tapioca | Tropical Tasar — Som and Soalu | Muga — Terminalia and Sal
  x: Eri silkworms feed on castor and tapioca; Tropical Tasar on Terminalia and Sal, Muga on Som and Soalu, and Mulberry on mulberry.
[nc87f583cb24] sub0
  Where is the headquarters of the Central Silk Board?
  *Bengaluru | Hyderabad | Cuttack | Karnal
  x: The headquarters of the Central Silk Board is at Bengaluru.
[nf588ac4287e] sub0
  In the life cycle of a honey bee, which stage comes immediately after the larva?
  Egg | Adult | *Pupa | Nymph
  x: Honey bees pass through egg, larva, pupa and adult, so the pupa follows the larva.
[n0254dce17d3] sub0
  Which of the following pairs is correctly matched?
  *Apiculture — Honey and bee-wax | Sericulture — Fish | Pisciculture — Silk | Floriculture — Lac
  x: Apiculture is the rearing of bees and yields honey and bee-wax. Sericulture yields silk, pisciculture yields fish and floriculture concerns flowers.
[f411262513f16] sub0
  On which plant do the silkworms of Eri silk, known as 'peace silk', feed?
  Mulberry | Oak | *Castor | Som and Soalu
  x: Eri silkworms feed on castor (and tapioca); muga feeds on som and soalu.
[fa6e4f10e619e] sub0
  On which plants do Muga silkworms feed?
  Castor | Terminalia | Mulberry | *Som and Soalu
  x: Muga feeds on som and soalu; tasar feeds on Terminalia and sal.
[faf2bae0424f0] sub0
  In which State is oak (temperate) tasar silk mainly produced?
  *Manipur | Karnataka | Assam | Andhra Pradesh
  x: Oak tasar comes from Manipur, Uttarakhand, HP and J&K.
[f1f2d3ae3d396] sub0
  What is reared in lac culture?
  Silkworms | *Lac insects | Honey bees | Fish
  x: Lac culture produces lac from lac insects.
[f87952883478a] sub0
  Consider the following statements about silk in India:
1. India is the only country producing all five silks.
2. India is the largest producer of raw silk in the world.
3. Karnataka is the largest silk-producing State.
Which of the statements given above is/are correct?
  1 and 2 only | 2 and 3 only | *1 and 3 only | 1, 2 and 3
  x: India produces all five silks and Karnataka leads. Statement 2 is wrong: India is 2nd after China.
[gba0694d11c5f] sub0

  x: AP's tasar silk comes from East Godavari and ASR (Rampachodavaram) districts.
[n06f3d80f522] sub1

  x: Barbari is a goat breed of Uttar Pradesh, not a poultry breed. Hallikar, Murrah and Jamunapari are correctly matched.
[n58ed126f986] sub1

  x: Ongole is a cattle breed native to the Prakasam district of Andhra Pradesh. Murrah is a buffalo, Kadaknath is poultry, and Jamunapari is a goat.

[n9f5a244181d] sub2
  What is the main aim of the Rashtriya Gokul Mission?
  *Development and conservation of indigenous bovine breeds | Expansion of inland fish-seed farms | Building milk- and meat-processing capacity | Promotion of sericulture in tribal areas
  x: The Rashtriya Gokul Mission focuses on indigenous breeds; AP's Gokul Gram at Chadalawada (Prakasam) is a cattle-breeding farm under it. Building milk- and meat-processing capacity is the job of the Animal Husbandry Infrastructure Development Fund; fish seed and sericulture belong to other schemes.
[n96000e53d81] sub2
  Which one of the following is described as the fastest-growing farm sub-sector, with approximately 8.8 per cent decadal growth?
  Forestry | Livestock | *Fisheries | Horticulture
  x: Fisheries is described as the fastest-growing farm sub-sector, with approximately 8.8 per cent decadal growth. Livestock growth is stated as about 7.1 per cent.
[n31067776072] sub2
  Which of the following is NOT correct about the growth of livestock and fisheries during FY15–FY24?
  Livestock GVA grew by about 195 percent | *Livestock and fisheries grew at identical rates of 8.8 percent | Livestock grew at about 7.1 percent over the decade | Fisheries grew at about 8.8 percent over the decade
  x: The rates were not identical: livestock grew about 7.1 percent and fisheries about 8.8 percent. Livestock GVA did grow about 195 percent.

=== NOTES 4:87 | India – Iron and Steel, Aluminium and Other Metallurgical Industries
## SUB 0 | Integrated Steel Plants – Year, Collaboration, Location
[87:0:0] TABLE head: Plant | State / water | Year / collaboration
  [87:0:0.0] TISCO, Jamshedpur (Tatanagar) | Jharkhand, East Singhbhum district; Subarnarekha–Kharkai confluence at Sakchi | 1907, private (Tata); oldest and largest
  [87:0:0.1] IISCO, Kulti-Burnpur (Asansol) | West Bengal; Barakar river | Pre-independence; now SAIL
  [87:0:0.2] VISL Bhadravati | Karnataka; Bhadra river | 1923; oldest public-sector plant in the south
  [87:0:0.3] Bhilai | Chhattisgarh (Durg); not on a river – water from Tandula dam | 1957; USSR (Russia)
  [87:0:0.4] Rourkela | Odisha (Sundargarh); Koel and Sankh | 1959; West Germany (Krupp and Demag); first public-sector integrated plant
  [87:0:0.5] Durgapur | West Bengal; Damodar (DVC) | 1959; UK (British)
  [87:0:0.6] Bokaro | Jharkhand; Damodar–Bokaro confluence | 1964; USSR
  [87:0:0.7] Visakhapatnam (RINL) | Andhra Pradesh | USSR help; first shore-based plant
  [87:0:0.8] Salem | Tamil Nadu | SAIL special steel
  [87:0:0.9] Vijayanagar | Karnataka (Hospet) | Placed away from raw materials
[87:0:1] - **First steel industry of India** (exam key): **Jamshedpur (TISCO, 1907)**; earlier: Porto Novo (TN) 1830 iron unit, Kulti 1874.
[87:0:2] - **Post-independence group** (Second Plan 1956-61): **Bhilai, Durgapur, Rourkela** – Jamshedpur, Bhadravati, Kulti-Burnpur are pre-1947.
[87:0:3] - **Pittsburgh of India**: **Jamshedpur**.
[87:0:4] - **SAIL**: incorporated **24 Jan 1973** (books/keys often give **1974**); runs Bhilai, Rourkela, Durgapur, Bokaro, Burnpur + special steel plants Salem, Durgapur (alloy), Bhadravati; HQ **New Delhi**.
[87:0:5] - **Prime Minister's Trophy** for best integrated steel plant (from 1992-93): **SAIL Bhilai** – record **11 wins**.
[87:0:6] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Geography 10 p.10; PMF Human & Economic Geography 05 p.16-22; app notes 4:87 sub 1
## SUB 1 | Locational Factors of Steel Plants
[87:1:0] - Raw materials are **weight-losing**, so plants sit on the **Chota Nagpur** coal-ore crescent (Jharkhand, N. Odisha, Chhattisgarh, W. Bengal).
[87:1:1] - **Near coalfields:** Bokaro, Durgapur, Kulti-Burnpur; **near iron ore:** Bhadravati, Bhilai, Rourkela.
[87:1:2] - **Far from coalfields:** **Bhadravati** (Kemmangundi ore; charcoal, then Sharavati/Jog hydel power).
[87:1:3] - **Midway** between ore (Noamundi, Gorumahisani) and coal (Jharia): **Jamshedpur**.
[87:1:4] - **Not on a riverside:** **Bhilai** (Tandula dam water); Bokaro (Damodar), Jamshedpur (Subarnarekha–Kharkai), Bhadravati (Bhadra) are.
[87:1:5] - **West-coast plants** (Ratnagiri, Mangalore, Dolvi, Hazira): exam reason = **high-grade iron ore of Goa** plus ports for imported coking coal and export.
[87:1:6] - **Coking coal** is the input India must **import** (UPSC 2015) – domestic coking coal is **high-ash**; not saltpetre or rock phosphate.
[87:1:7] - Plants put away from raw materials for regional balance: **Salem, Visakhapatnam, Vijayanagar**.
[87:1:8] - **Bokaro–Rourkela combine**: Bokaro gets iron ore from Rourkela and sends back coal.
[87:1:9] ~~ Sources: PMF Human & Economic Geography 05 p.11, 16-22, 25; app notes 4:87 sub 2
## SUB 2 | Output, Rank and Steel Policy
[87:2:0] - India = **2nd largest crude steel producer** after **China** (China over 50% of world steel).
[87:2:1] TABLE head: FY | Crude steel (MT)
  [87:2:1.0] 2023-24 | 144.3
  [87:2:1.1] 2024-25 | 152.2
  [87:2:1.2] 2025-26 | 168.4 (+10.7%; about 9% CAGR since 2021-22)
[87:2:2] - **APPSC G1 2023 statements:** India 2nd ✓; India 1st ✗; output in **2020 fell** below 2019 (COVID) ✗ → answer **I and II**.
[87:2:3] - 168.4 MT = 2025-26 output; **NSP 2017**: 300 MT capacity and 255 MT production by 2030-31 (437 MT ore + 161 MT coking coal).
[87:2:4] - **Mission Purvodaya** (2020): develop **Eastern India as an integrated steel hub**.
[87:2:5] - **PLI for Specialty Steel**: approved 2021. **Green Hydrogen Mission**: ₹455 crore for steel pilots till 2029-30.
[87:2:6] - Do not confuse: ₹25,938 crore = PLI-Auto; ₹33,660 crore = BHAVYA; ₹3,000 crore = NICDIT (BE 2026-27).
[87:2:7] - Emissions: Indian steel **2.54 t CO2/t** vs world **1.9**; 10–12% of national CO2; EU CBAM exposure.
[87:2:8] - Draft notification brings iron and steel under the **Carbon Credit Trading Scheme**: cuts for 255 plants from FY 2026-27 (19 Jul 2026).
[87:2:9] ~~ Sources: PMF Human & Economic Geography 05 p.23; app notes 4:87 sub 0, 4
## SUB 3 | Iron and Steel – Types, Raw Materials and Process
[87:3:0] TABLE head: Form | Carbon | Note
  [87:3:0.0] Pig iron | ~4% | Blast-furnace product, brittle
  [87:3:0.1] Cast iron | ~3% | Hard, brittle
  [87:3:0.2] Wrought iron | < 0.1% | Purest commercial iron
  [87:3:0.3] Mild steel | 0.1–0.4% | —
  [87:3:0.4] Hard (high-carbon) steel | 0.5–1.5% | —
[87:3:1] - Blast furnace: coke (fuel, reducer) + iron ore + limestone (flux).
[87:3:2] - Process mix (8 Sep 2026): **43% BF-BOF, 22% EAF, 35% induction furnace**; BF-BOF projected at 56% by 2030.
[87:3:3] - Mini/secondary units melt scrap or **sponge iron** in EAF and induction furnaces.
[87:3:4] - India = **leading producer of sponge iron (DRI)**; **Odisha** 1st, **Chhattisgarh** 2nd, then West Bengal and Jharkhand.
[87:3:5] - 2024-25: iron ore output 289.398 MT (imports 6.4 MT); coking coal output 66.49 MT but imports **57.07 MT**.
[87:3:6] - Old framing (IAS 1993): India self-sufficient in **coking coal** for three decades – now outdated (heavy imports).
[87:3:7] ~~ Sources: PMF Human & Economic Geography 05 p.13-15; NextGen Indian Geography 10 p.10; app notes 4:87 sub 3
## SUB 4 | Vizag Steel – AP Angle
[87:4:0] - **Visakhapatnam Steel Plant (RINL)**: built with **USSR** technical help; India's first **shore-based** integrated plant.
[87:4:1] - 1965-66 agitation slogan: "**Visakha Steel – Andhra's Right**".
[87:4:2] - Chosen over Bailadila, Goa, Neyveli, Salem and Hospet (not Durgapur).
[87:4:3] - Foundation stone **20 January 1971**; first blast furnace named **Godavari** (foundation 1982).
[87:4:4] - Explosion killed 8 (8 Jun 2026); 3-member probe panel (10 Jun 2026).
[87:4:5] - Hindustan Zinc runs a **zinc smelter** at Visakhapatnam.
[87:4:6] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Geography 10 p.10; app notes 4:87 sub 5
## SUB 5 | Aluminium – Companies and Plants
[87:5:0] - **2nd most important** metallurgical industry after steel; India **2nd** in primary aluminium; **7th** in bauxite reserves.
[87:5:1] - **Bauxite** is the principal raw material; **Odisha** = largest reserves and production (Koraput, Kalahandi).
[87:5:2] - **Power-oriented**: about **14,000–15,000 kWh per tonne** → smelters near hydel or captive thermal power.
[87:5:3] TABLE head: Company | Location | Note
  [87:5:3.0] NALCO | Refinery Damanjodi (Koraput); smelter Angul; HQ Bhubaneswar | Panchpatmali bauxite; French help; largest complex
  [87:5:3.1] Vedanta | Jharsuguda, Odisha | Largest smelter
  [87:5:3.2] BALCO | Korba, Chhattisgarh (not Raipur) | Amarkantak bauxite
  [87:5:3.3] HINDALCO | Renukoot (Pipri, near Rihand), Mirzapur, UP | Top seller; Lohardaga bauxite
  [87:5:3.4] INDAL (Indian Aluminium Co.) | Muri (Jharkhand), Hirakud smelter (Odisha), Alupuram (Kerala), Belur | –
  [87:5:3.5] MALCO | Mettur, TN | –
[87:5:4] - **UPSC 2007 match:** BALCO–Korba, HINDALCO–Renukoot, INDAL–Hirakud, NALCO–Koraput.
[87:5:5] - **TELCO** (Tata Motors) makes vehicles, **not aluminium**.
[87:5:6] - Sales order: HINDALCO > NALCO > INDAL > MALCO.
[87:5:7] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Geography 10 p.11; PMF Human & Economic Geography 06 p.21, 57-59; app notes 4:87 sub 6
## SUB 6 | Copper, Zinc, Lead and Metal PSUs
[87:6:0] TABLE head: Centre | State | Metal / operator
  [87:6:0.0] Khetri | Rajasthan | Copper, HCL (Hindustan Copper)
  [87:6:0.1] Ghatsila / Mosabani | Jharkhand | Copper, HCL
  [87:6:0.2] Malanjkhand | MP | Largest open-pit copper mine, HCL
  [87:6:0.3] Dahej | Gujarat | Copper, HINDALCO, imported concentrate
  [87:6:0.4] Thoothukudi | TN | Sterlite copper – shut
  [87:6:0.5] Zawar | Rajasthan | Lead-zinc, Hindustan Zinc
  [87:6:0.6] Visakhapatnam | AP | Zinc smelter, Hindustan Zinc
[87:6:1] - **Rampur Agucha** (**Bhilwara**, Rajasthan): Asia's best zinc-lead reserves; Rajasthan near-monopoly in zinc and lead.
[87:6:2] - **PSU match (UP 2010):** SAIL–Delhi, **Hindustan Zinc–Udaipur**, **HEC–Ranchi** (1958), **Instrumentation Ltd–Kota** → 4 2 3 1.
[87:6:3] - Copper: India under **2%** of reserves, net importer of concentrate; **Chile** leads.
[87:6:4] ~~ Sources: NextGen Rocket Indian Geography #8 p.27-28; NextGen Indian Geography 10 p.11; app notes 4:87 sub 7

=== OWN MCQs of 4:87 (83)
[fce8268104bf5] sub0
  The Bhilai Steel Plant stands on the banks of which river?
  Damodar | Sheonath | Subarnarekha | Kharkai | *None of these – it draws water from the Tandula dam
  x: The printed options did not include the correct answer (None of these – it draws water from the Tandula dam); it is added as option 5.
Bhilai is not a riverside plant: PMF records its water comes from the Tandula dam (UPRO 2021 keys Bhilai as the plant 'not located on a riverside'). The Damodar serves Bokaro, and the Subarnarekha and Kharkai serve TISCO; the Sheonath flows past Durg town, not the plant.
[f2edc91267f4c] sub0
  Which of the following pairs of steel plant and collaborating country is INCORRECTLY matched?
  Rourkela – Germany | Bhilai – USSR | *Durgapur – USSR | Bokaro – USSR
  x: Durgapur was built with UK (British) collaboration; Rourkela was German and Bhilai and Bokaro were USSR plants.
[f09bcfd4227ca] sub0
  TISCO, Jamshedpur stands at the confluence of which rivers?
  Damodar and Bokaro | Damodar and Barakar | Sheonath and Mahanadi | *Subarnarekha and Kharkai
  x: TISCO (1907) is at Sakchi, at the confluence of the Subarnarekha and Kharkai; Bokaro is at the Damodar–Bokaro confluence.
[f126e5fe005e0] sub0
  Which was India's first public-sector integrated steel plant?
  *Rourkela | Bhilai | Durgapur | Bokaro
  x: Rourkela (Odisha), built with German help, was the first public-sector integrated plant.
[fc0aa50efbcf9] sub0
  In which year was the Prime Minister's Trophy for the best integrated steel plant instituted?
  1982-83 | *1992-93 | 2002-03 | 2014-15
  x: The trophy was instituted in 1992-93; SAIL Bhilai holds the record with 11 wins.

=== NOTES 4:88 | India – Cotton Textiles, Jute, Sugar, Cement, Paper, Fertiliser and Petrochemical Industries
## SUB 0 | Cotton Textiles – Oldest Industry, Centres, Nicknames and Location
[88:0:0] # History and status
[88:0:1] - **Oldest / ancient industry of India:** **cotton textiles** (muslin, calico, chintz were famous worldwide) – older than jute, tea or sugar, which are modern mill industries (UPPSC 2004 twice).
[88:0:2] - **First cotton mill:** **1818, Fort Gloster near Kolkata (Calcutta)** – a commercial failure (UPUDA 2013; Jharkhand 2013).
[88:0:3] - **First successful mill:** **1854, Mumbai**, by Parsi entrepreneur **C.N. Davar** (local capital); growth surged after the American Civil War (1860s-70s).
[88:0:4] - **Only major industry in Indian hands at the end of the 19th century:** **cotton textiles** – jute, mining and tea were British-owned; Indian-owned steel began only with TISCO (1907).
[88:0:5] - **Largest organised modern industry of India:** cotton textiles; three tiers – handspun khadi, handloom/powerloom, and the capital-intensive mill sector (powerlooms now make most cloth).
[88:0:6] # Location and dispersal
[88:0:7] - Began in the **black-soil cotton belt of Maharashtra and Gujarat**; **Bombay–Ahmedabad** = the most prominent cotton textile region (APPSC G-I 2003).
[88:0:8] - **Dispersed** all over India because raw cotton is a **pure raw material that does not lose weight** in manufacture, so mills followed markets, ports, power and cheap labour (Kanpur, Kolkata, Coimbatore) (RAS 2013 A-R: both correct).
[88:0:9] - **Tamil Nadu – mill-made yarn:** hydel power, **rich pool of skilled/cheap labour**, local cotton; **black cotton soil is NOT predominant** in TN (UPSC 2010: only II).
[88:0:10] - **Largest number of mills:** **Tamil Nadu** (most produce yarn, Coimbatore has nearly half) – older books and keys say **Maharashtra** (Mumbai the largest centre).
[88:0:11] - **Not a cotton region:** **Dhanbad–Jamshedpur** = coal and steel; Mumbai–Pune, Madurai–Coimbatore, Indore–Ujjain are cotton regions (NDA 2017).
[88:0:12] - **Raw cotton exports:** India's largest buyers are **Bangladesh and China**, not South Korea.
[88:0:13] TABLE head: Nickname | City
  [88:0:13.0] Manchester of India | Ahmedabad (asked 3 times)
  [88:0:13.1] Cottonopolis (largest centre) | Mumbai
  [88:0:13.2] Manchester of South India | Coimbatore
  [88:0:13.3] Manchester of the North | Kanpur
  [88:0:13.4] Pittsburgh of India / Steel City | Jamshedpur
[88:0:14] # Allied centres
[88:0:15] - **Hosiery:** **Ludhiana** (Punjab's industrial capital; also Avon/Hero cycles). **Kota** = synthetic silk. Woollens: Kanpur (first woollen mill 1876), Dhariwal, Ludhiana, Jamnagar, Srinagar.
[88:0:16] - **Handloom and prints:** **Chanderi sarees – Madhya Pradesh**; **Nandana block print – Tarapur village, Madhya Pradesh**.
[88:0:17] > Statement angle (Sachivalayam 2019): "cotton textile = largest organised modern industry" ✓; first mill in New Delhi ✗; South Korea biggest importer ✗.
[88:0:18] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Geography - APPSC Exams Part 7.2 p.8 (book p.134); PMF Human & Economic Geography Part 5 p.38-41; Rocket Indian Geography p.94; app notes 4:88 sub 0-1
## SUB 1 | Sugar Industry – Producers, Belts, North–South Shift and Policy
[88:1:0] # World and India
[88:1:1] - **Largest producer:** **Brazil**; India **2nd** (~19%) and **largest consumer**; India overtook Brazil only in **2021-22** (first time in 16 years). Brazil is also the largest exporter.
[88:1:2] - **"Sugar Bowl of the world" = Cuba** (largest producer and exporter till the 1960s); **"Sugar Bowl of India" = Uttar Pradesh**.
[88:1:3] - **2nd largest agro-based industry** of India after cotton textiles; mills sit near cane because cane is **bulky, perishable and weight-losing** (sugar only ~10% of cane).
[88:1:4] # States
[88:1:5] TABLE head: State | Position
  [88:1:5.0] Maharashtra | Largest producer in 2015-16 and in the latest seasons (ISMA, Dec 2025)
  [88:1:5.1] Uttar Pradesh | Largest cane producer; largest long-run share (~35%) and most sugar factories among UP, TN, Bihar, Assam; now 2nd in sugar
  [88:1:5.2] Karnataka | 3rd
  [88:1:5.3] Tamil Nadu | 4th
[88:1:6] - **Top three:** **UP, Maharashtra, Karnataka** (order now Maharashtra > UP > Karnataka; 2015-16 descending: **Maharashtra, UP, Karnataka, Tamil Nadu**).
[88:1:7] - **Belts:** western UP (Meerut-Saharanpur-Muzaffarnagar), eastern UP (Gorakhpur-Deoria-Basti); Maharashtra cooperatives **Nasik, Pune, Satara, Sangli, Kolhapur, Sholapur**; AP: East and West Godavari, Visakhapatnam, Chittoor.
[88:1:8] - **Sakkar (Shakkar) Nagar:** near **Bodhan (Nizamabad)**, around the Nizam Sugar Factory – in undivided **Andhra Pradesh**, **now Telangana**.
[88:1:9] - **First sugar mill:** **1903, Pratappur (Deoria, UP)**.
[88:1:10] # North → South shift
[88:1:11] TABLE head: North (UP, Bihar) | South (Maharashtra, Karnataka, TN, AP)
  [88:1:11.0] Subtropical cane, lower sucrose and yield | Tropical cane, higher sucrose and higher yield per acre
  [88:1:11.1] Crushing season Nov–Feb (~4 months) | Longer crushing season (Oct to May-June)
  [88:1:11.2] More but smaller, older mills | Larger, modern, better-run cooperatives
[88:1:12] - **Reasons for the shift:** higher yield, higher sucrose, longer crushing season – **not** cheap labour, power or railways (OPSC 2018; UPPSC 2007).
[88:1:13] # Policy and by-products
[88:1:14] - **Rangarajan Committee (2012):** **decontrol** – end levy sugar and regulated release; **70:30** revenue sharing. Govt **partially decontrolled** sugar in **April 2013** (millers free to sell in the open market, no levy obligation to ration shops) (CDS 2013: both statements correct).
[88:1:15] - **By-products:** bagasse – boiler fuel and paper pulp; molasses – alcohol/ethanol; press mud – manure. **FRP** = Centre's cane price; **SAP** = State price.
[88:1:16] - About **3/4 of ethanol** now from grain (app notes, 2026).
[88:1:17] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Geography Part 7.2 p.9-10 (book p.135-136); PMF Human & Economic Geography Part 5 p.61-64; Rocket Indian Geography p.94; app notes 4:88 sub 3 (ISMA Dec 2025)
## SUB 2 | Cement – Raw Materials, Firsts, Centres and Production
[88:2:0] - **Raw materials:** **limestone** (main), **clay** (silica, alumina) and **gypsum**; coal for fuel – so plants sit in limestone areas. **Mortar** = **sand + cement** (+ water) for brick walls.
[88:2:1] - **First attempt:** **1904, Madras (Chennai)**, sea shells as lime – failed; **first successful:** **Indian Cement Co., Porbandar**, set up **1912-13**, production **1914**. Exams answer "first cement factory = Chennai".
[88:2:2] - **World rank:** India **2nd** after China (also in 2010); per capita ~290 kg vs world ~540 kg.
[88:2:3] - **Largest state (2026):** Rajasthan, then AP (older books: AP first).
[88:2:4] TABLE head: Centre | State
  [88:2:4.0] Dalmianagar | Bihar
  [88:2:4.1] Chunar, Churk, Dalla, Dadri | Uttar Pradesh
  [88:2:4.2] Lakheri, Udaipur, Chittorgarh | Rajasthan
  [88:2:4.3] Satna, Katni | Madhya Pradesh
  [88:2:4.4] Kesoram Cements, Basantnagar | Karimnagar district of undivided AP (now Peddapalli, Telangana)
  [88:2:4.5] Nalgonda | Telangana
  [88:2:4.6] Yerraguntla (Kadapa), Jaggayyapeta (NTR), Nandyal | Andhra Pradesh (Kadapa–Kurnool limestone)
[88:2:5] - **Green cements:** PPC (fly ash) and PSC (steel slag).
[88:2:6] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Geography Part 7.2 p.13 (book p.139); OnlyIAS NCERT Human Geography Part 7 p.13; Rocket Indian Geography p.93; app notes 4:88 sub 0, 4
## SUB 3 | Fertilisers, Petrochemicals and Refineries
[88:3:0] # Fertilisers
[88:3:1] - **First super-phosphate factory:** **Ranipet (TN), 1906**. **First large-scale fertiliser plant:** **FACT, Alwaye/Udyogamandal (Kerala)** – incorporated 1943, production **1947**. **Sindri (1951)** = first public-sector plant of independent India (many books call Sindri "the first").
[88:3:2] - **Raw materials:** naphtha, natural gas, coal, **rock phosphate**, **sulphur**, **gypsum** – **caustic soda is NOT** one. Urea = ammonia (Haber) + CO2; superphosphate = rock phosphate + sulphuric acid.
[88:3:3] - **Biggest cooperative plant:** **Phulpur (Prayagraj, UP)** – IFFCO. Other centres: Sri Ganganagar, **Haldia**, Kota, Nangal, Kakinada.
[88:3:4] - **Gujarat** = largest fertiliser capacity; UP = largest urea producer. Natural gas/sulphur: sulphur for phosphatic fertiliser mainly from **oil refineries**.
[88:3:5] TABLE head: Region | Centres
  [88:3:5.0] Gujarat–Maharashtra (largest) | Vadodara, Kalol, Kandla, Trombay
  [88:3:5.1] TN–Kerala | Neyveli, Tuticorin, Ennore, Alwaye, Kochi
  [88:3:5.2] North-West | Nangal, Bhatinda, Panipat
  [88:3:5.3] Chota Nagpur steel towns | Sindri, Jamshedpur, Rourkela, Durgapur, Bhilai
  [88:3:5.4] Coal-based FCI units | Talcher, Ramagundam, Korba
  [88:3:5.5] AP | Kakinada (Nagarjuna, Coromandel)
[88:3:6] # Petrochemicals and refineries
[88:3:7] - **Largest petrochemical centre:** **Jamnagar, Gujarat** (Reliance; world's largest refining complex); others Vadodara (Koyali), Hazira, Ankleshwar, Trombay.
[88:3:8] - **Bhopal** (MP) = pesticides (Union Carbide plant, 1984 gas tragedy).
[88:3:9] - **Most westward petrochemical place (CDS 2010):** key **Kota** (75.8°E); Nagpur 79°E, Barauni 86°E – Ujjain (75.78°E) is marginally west of Kota.
[88:3:10] - **Refineries in AP = 2:** **HPCL Visakhapatnam** and **ONGC mini refinery at Tatipaka (Konaseema)**.
[88:3:11] TABLE head: Refinery | Fact
  [88:3:11.0] Digboi (Assam) | Oldest, 1901
  [88:3:11.1] Barauni (Bihar) | IOCL, 1964
  [88:3:11.2] Mathura (UP) | IOCL, 1982
  [88:3:11.3] Panipat (Haryana) | IOCL, 1998
  [88:3:11.4] Numaligarh (Assam) | NRL
  [88:3:11.5] Visakhapatnam (AP) | HPCL
  [88:3:11.6] Jamnagar (Gujarat) | Reliance, 1999
[88:3:12] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Geography Part 7.2 p.12 (book p.138); Rocket Indian Geography p.91-93; fact.co.in (FACT 1943/1947); app notes 4:88 sub 6
## SUB 4 | Jute – Hooghly Belt, Retting and Andhra Mills
[88:4:0] - **First jute mill:** **Rishra, near Kolkata, 1855**; jute is India's 2nd textile industry after cotton.
[88:4:1] - **Location:** ~**90%** of capacity in a **100 km x 3 km belt along the Hooghly** – most mills in the **Hugli basin**, **West Bengal** (APPSC G-I 2024).
[88:4:2] - **Why Hooghly:** raw jute of the delta, **soft/standing water for retting**, NW-1 and **Kolkata port**, coal from Raniganj, cheap labour, capital.
[88:4:3] - **Retting:** soaking jute stems in water so microbes loosen the fibre – the process that separates fibre from the stem.
[88:4:4] - **Mills:** WB 71 of 97 (2026); **AP 2nd** – Chittivalasa, Nellimarla; **Bhajrang Jute Mill, Guntur** – shut down, a long labour/management dispute.
[88:4:5] - **Bhatpara** = jute centre (Titagarh = paper). **National Jute Policy 2005**; **JUTE-ICARE** = certified seed + microbial retting with **SONA**.
[88:4:6] - **Largest exporter:** Bangladesh. **Jute Packaging Materials Act 1987:** 100% foodgrains, 20% sugar in jute bags.
[88:4:7] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Geography Part 7.2 p.9 (book p.135); PMF Human & Economic Geography Part 5 p.50-51; thenewsminute.com (Bhajrang Jute Mill shutdown); app notes 4:88 sub 2
## SUB 5 | Paper and Newsprint
[88:5:0] - **Raw materials:** bamboo (60-70%), sabai grass, bagasse, **poplar**, eucalyptus, rags, waste paper.
[88:5:1] - **Newsprint:** **Nepanagar (Burhanpur, MP)** (asked 2 times).
[88:5:2] - **Most paper mills:** **Gujarat**, then **Uttar Pradesh** (UP = most among Karnataka, MP, UP, WB); Maharashtra = largest producer.
[88:5:3] - **Jagiroad (Assam)** = Hindustan Paper Corporation mill (shut 2017). Paper uses no rock phosphate.
[88:5:4] - **Mills:** Titagarh (WB), Sirpur-Kagaznagar (Telangana), Rajahmundry (AP), Ballarpur (Maharashtra).
[88:5:5] - **Asia Pulp & Paper (APP, Indonesia's Sinar Mas)** announced a pulp-and-paper venture in AP (Prakasam) after bifurcation.
[88:5:6] ~~ Sources: PMF Human & Economic Geography Part 5 p.90-91; NextGen Indian Geography Part 7.2 p.14 (book p.140); capexil.org paper-and-paper-products (mill count); app notes 4:88 sub 5

## SUB 7 | First Modern Industrial Units – Chronology
[88:7:0] - **First industry to develop in India:** the **cottage industry**.
[88:7:1] TABLE head: Unit | Place | Year
  [88:7:1.0] Paper mill | Serampore (WB) | 1812
  [88:7:1.1] Cotton mill (failed) | Fort Gloster, near Kolkata | 1818
  [88:7:1.2] Iron unit on modern lines | Porto Novo (TN) | 1830
  [88:7:1.3] First successful cotton mill | Mumbai – C.N. Davar | 1854
  [88:7:1.4] Jute mill | Rishra, near Kolkata | 1855
  [88:7:1.5] Steel plant | Bengal Iron Works Company | 1870
  [88:7:1.6] Woollen mill | Kanpur (army depot) | 1876
  [88:7:1.7] Sugar mill | Pratappur, Deoria (UP) | 1903
  [88:7:1.8] Cement (failed) | Madras, sea shells as lime | 1904
  [88:7:1.9] Super-phosphate factory | Ranipet (TN) | 1906
  [88:7:1.10] Integrated steel | TISCO, Jamshedpur | 1907
  [88:7:1.11] First successful cement | Indian Cement Co., Porbandar | 1912-13 (production 1914)
  [88:7:1.12] Aircraft factory | Bengaluru (Hindustan Aircraft) | 1940
  [88:7:1.13] First fertiliser PSU | Sindri (Jharkhand) | 1951
[88:7:2] - **Sequence:** Serampore paper → Ranipet super-phosphate → TISCO → Bengaluru aircraft.
[88:7:3] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Geography Part 7.2 p.8-13 (book p.134-139); app notes 4:88 sub 0

  x: The northern season is November to February. The North has many private mills (Bihar mostly non-cooperative); cooperatives are strong in the South, especially Maharashtra.

  x: Portland Pozzolana Cement (PPC) uses fly ash from thermal power plants, and Portland Slag Cement (PSC) uses slag from steel plants. So the pair is fly ash and slag.

  x: Urea is made from ammonia (via the Haber process) and carbon dioxide. Rock phosphate with sulphuric acid makes superphosphate.

=== NOTES 4:89 | India – Automobile, Engineering, Electronics, Pharmaceutical and It Industries
## SUB 0 | Railway Production Units
[89:0:0] TABLE head: Unit | Location | Product
  [89:0:0.0] ICF | Perambur, Chennai (Tamil Nadu) | Passenger coaches; 1955, Swiss collaboration
  [89:0:0.1] RCF | Kapurthala (Punjab) | Passenger coaches; 1988
  [89:0:0.2] MCF (Modern Coach Factory) | Rae Bareli (UP) | Third coach factory; foundation 2007, work from 2009
  [89:0:0.3] CLW | Chittaranjan (WB) | Electric locomotives
  [89:0:0.4] BLW (ex-DLW) | Varanasi (Manduadih) | Diesel-electric locomotives; now electric locomotives (renamed Banaras Locomotive Works)
  [89:0:0.5] PLW (ex-DMW, Diesel Loco Modernisation Works) | Patiala | Locomotives
  [89:0:0.6] RWF | Yelahanka, Bengaluru | Wheels and axles; 1984
[89:0:1] - **Coach states:** **Punjab (Kapurthala) and Tamil Nadu (Perambur)**; UP (Rae Bareli) newer.
[89:0:2] - **Diesel locomotives = Varanasi**, not Chittaranjan (electric) or Perambur (coaches).
[89:0:3] - **Wagons:** mostly private; about **60% from West Bengal**; in 2021 India's ambassador sought export of Indian wagons to **Brazil** (printed key). TELCO Jamshedpur once made locomotives.
[89:0:4] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Geography Part 7.2 p.11 (book p.137); app notes 4:89 sub 1
## SUB 1 | Shipbuilding and Ship-breaking
[89:1:0] TABLE head: Yard | Location | Note
  [89:1:0.0] Cochin Shipyard | Kochi (Kerala) | Largest shipyard (asked 2 times)
  [89:1:0.1] Hindustan Shipyard | Visakhapatnam, AP | Oldest (1941)
  [89:1:0.2] Mazagon Dock | Mumbai | Defence PSU
  [89:1:0.3] GRSE | Kolkata | Its diesel engine plant is at Ranchi
  [89:1:0.4] Goa Shipyard | Goa | Defence
  [89:1:0.5] Alang-Sosiya | Bhavnagar, Gujarat | Largest ship-breaking yard (Greenpeace's Rainbow Warrior campaign, Mumbai)
[89:1:1] - **Reliance Defence** signed an MoU with AP on **10 Jan 2016** for a naval facility (shipyard) at **Rambilli** near Visakhapatnam (₹5,000 crore).
[89:1:2] - **World:** **South Korea** = largest shipbuilder by value when asked (China, Japan follow).
[89:1:3] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Geography Part 7.2 p.12 (book p.138); business-standard.com Reuters 10 Jan 2016 (Rambilli); app notes 4:89 sub 2
## SUB 2 | Heavy Engineering, Electricals and Defence Units
[89:2:0] TABLE head: Unit | Location | Product
  [89:2:0.0] HEC | Ranchi, 1958 | First heavy-machinery unit
  [89:2:0.1] HMT | Bengaluru, Pinjore, Kalamassery, Hyderabad, Srinagar, Ajmer | Largest machine-tool maker
  [89:2:0.2] BHEL | Bhopal, Hyderabad (Ramachandrapuram), Tiruchirappalli, Haridwar, Jhansi, Ranipet | Heavy electricals; Bhopal – traction motors; Jhansi – electric locomotives
  [89:2:0.3] BEML (Bharat Earth Movers) | Bengaluru | Earth movers, rail coaches
  [89:2:0.4] HAL | Bengaluru (Hindustan Aircraft 1940; HAL 1964) | Nashik – MiG airframe; Koraput – MiG engines; Kanpur – transport aircraft
  [89:2:0.5] BEL | Bengaluru | Defence electronics; Rajiv Centres of the NGO TIMES set up through BEL (G-II 2011, printed key)
[89:2:1] - **Pinjore and Srinagar = HMT**, not BHEL.
[89:2:2] - **Ordnance Factory Board (41 units) → 7 DPSUs** (not BEL). Defence production FY 2024-25: **₹1,51,339 crore**. Private defence opened **May 2001** (FDI 26%).
[89:2:3] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Geography Part 7.2 p.11-12 (book p.137-138); app notes 4:89 sub 0
## SUB 3 | IT and Software
[89:3:0] - IT is **footloose**: pulled by engineering talent and a tech ecosystem, not raw materials. **STPI** set up **1991**.
[89:3:1] - **Bengaluru = "Silicon Valley of India"** and the city with the greatest IT capacity; **Karnataka = "Silicon State"** and the largest software exporter; **Hyderabad = "Cyberabad"**.
[89:3:2] ~~ Sources: app notes 4:89 sub 6

## SUB 5 | Electronics, Semiconductors and Pharmaceuticals
[89:5:0] - **India Semiconductor Mission:** 2021. **Dholera** = fab; **Sanand** = Micron ATMP; **Jagiroad** (Assam) = Tata ₹27,000-crore plant (Mar 2024).
[89:5:1] - Electronics PLI grew out of **National Policy on Electronics 2019**. AP electronics target (2024-29): **US$50 bn**.
[89:5:2] - **Pharmacy of the World:** **3rd by volume, 11th by value**. Hubs: **Hyderabad (bulk drugs)**, Ahmedabad-Vadodara, **Parawada Pharma City (Visakhapatnam)**. **Bulk Drug Parks:** AP, Gujarat, Himachal Pradesh.
[89:5:3] ~~ Sources: app notes 4:89 sub 5, 7

=== NOTES 4:90 | Classification of Basic Industries – Agro, Mineral, Forest, Fuel and Manpower-based
## SUB 0 | Classification by Raw Material – Agro, Mineral, Forest, Animal, Marine
[90:0:0] TABLE head: Class | Features | Examples
  [90:0:0.0] Agro-based | Farm produce; near raw material (perishable, weight-losing) | Cotton textile, jute, sugar, vanaspati, cigarettes, tea, paper (bagasse, bamboo)
  [90:0:0.1] Mineral-based | Capital-intensive; near mines | Iron and steel, aluminium, cement, copper smelting
  [90:0:0.2] Forest-based | Timber + minor forest produce | Pulp and paper (largest), furniture, plywood, matchsticks, lac, rayon
  [90:0:0.3] Animal-based | Animal produce | Dairy, leather, wool
  [90:0:0.4] Marine-based | Sea produce | Seafood and coastal aquaculture processing
[90:0:1] - **Cement is NOT agro-based** (limestone = mineral) (APPSC G-I 2008).
[90:0:2] - **Rayon = "artificial silk"**, made from **wood pulp** (cellulose) (APPSC G-I 2002).
[90:0:3] - **Pollutant–industry:** **bleaching liquor – paper and pulp** ✓; cellulose fibres are not a pharma pollutant, drugs/antibiotics not a mining pollutant (SSC 2017: only A).
[90:0:4] - **Leather:** UP State Leather Development and Marketing Corporation – **Agra** (printed key). AP: Nellore – leather, auto parts.
[90:0:5] ~~ Sources: OnlyIAS NCERT Human Geography Part 7 p.13; app notes 4:90 sub 0, 2
## SUB 1 | Fuel-based and Manpower-based Industries; Water and Coal Use
[90:1:0] TABLE head: Class | Basis | Examples
  [90:1:0.0] Fuel-based (energy-based) | Coal, petroleum, gas, nuclear | Thermal power, oil refining, petrochemicals, gas-based fertilisers
  [90:1:0.1] Manpower-based (labour-intensive) | High human labour | Handloom, handicrafts, leather, garments
[90:1:1] - **Maximum industrial water consumer = thermal power** (~**88%**); engineering ~5%, paper ~2.3%, textiles ~2% (UPSC 2013).
[90:1:2] - **Maximum coal use = energy (power) production**, not steel, railways or ships.
[90:1:3] - AP: Mallavalli (Krishna) Mega Food Park; Prakasam, Kurnool – mineral-based.
[90:1:4] ~~ Sources: app notes 4:90 sub 1-2
## SUB 2 | Paper, Pulp and Timber – World Leaders
[90:2:0] - **Pulp and paper "most developed":** **USA and Canada** (softwood, hydel power, water); **Canada** exports wood pulp to the USA, Europe, Asia. The APPSC 2002 question was keyed both ways (Canada; **Mexico**).
[90:2:1] - **Soft timber and wood pulp (when asked):** **Canada** largest producer and exporter. **Now (FAO 2022):** pulp – producer **USA (23.5%)**, exporter **Brazil (29%)**; sawn wood exporter **Russia (20%)**, Canada 2nd.
[90:2:2] - **Pulp woods:** **poplar** (woody), also eucalyptus, bamboo; bagasse and rice straw are non-woody. Recycled fibre 71-75% of raw material.
[90:2:3] - Paper is **capital-intensive**; top paper exporters Finland, Sweden, Canada; China = largest furniture exporter; rayon led by China, India, Indonesia. India per capita paper use ~16 kg (world 57).
[90:2:4] ~~ Sources: PMF Human & Economic Geography Part 5 p.86, p.91; app notes 4:90 sub 7
## SUB 3 | Ownership – Public, Private, Joint, Cooperative
[90:3:0] TABLE head: Sector | Examples
  [90:3:0.0] Public | SAIL, BHEL, HAL, RINL, NALCO
  [90:3:0.1] Private | TISCO, Reliance, JSW, Bajaj Auto
  [90:3:0.2] Joint | Maruti Udyog (GoI + Suzuki); APIIC joint ventures
  [90:3:0.3] Cooperative | Amul (Anand), Sudha Dairy; Maharashtra sugar mills
[90:3:1] - **Maruti Udyog:** incorporated **Feb 1981** as a Government company; JV with **Suzuki (26%)** in **1982**, so **GoI 74%** (the answer exams give); Suzuki 40% (1987), 50% (1992).
[90:3:2] - **Industrial Policy Resolution 1956:** **Schedule A** (arms, atomic energy, iron and steel) = public sector; **Schedule B** = both.
[90:3:3] - **Salem (TN):** SAIL's **stainless steel** plant – Salem is famous for **steel** (and magnesite).
[90:3:4] ~~ Sources: OnlyIAS NCERT Human Geography Part 7 p.14; web search (en.wikipedia.org Maruti Suzuki: GoI 74%, Suzuki 26% in 1982); app notes 4:90 sub 3
## SUB 4 | MSMEs and Use-based Classes
[90:4:0] - **Ministry of MSME (2007)** = merger of Ministry of Small Scale Industries + Ministry of Agro and Rural Industries. **2020 definition:** composite **investment + turnover**, no manufacturing/services difference.
[90:4:1] TABLE head: Class (from 1 Apr 2025) | Investment up to | Turnover up to
  [90:4:1.0] Micro | ₹2.5 crore | ₹10 crore
  [90:4:1.1] Small | ₹25 crore | ₹100 crore
  [90:4:1.2] Medium | ₹125 crore | ₹500 crore
[90:4:2] - **IIP use-based classes (six):** primary, capital, intermediate, infrastructure/construction, consumer durables (appliances), consumer non-durables – **no "export goods"**.
[90:4:3] TABLE head: Term | Meaning / examples
  [90:4:3.0] Basic / primary goods | Steel, cement, coal
  [90:4:3.1] Heavy | Bulky inputs and outputs, capital-intensive: iron and steel, HEC Ranchi
  [90:4:3.2] Light | Electronics assembly, garments
  [90:4:3.3] Footloose | Diamond polishing, software
  [90:4:3.4] Sunrise / sunset | AI, green hydrogen, EVs, semiconductors / legacy print
[90:4:4] - **Quinary** sector = top decision-making, **"gold collar"**.
[90:4:5] ~~ Sources: app notes 4:90 sub 4-5
## SUB 5 | Index of Core Industries
[90:5:0] - **ICI rebased** to **2022-23** (July 2026; IIP also rebased). **Iron ore** added: now **9** industries; textiles are not in it.
[90:5:1] TABLE head: Industry | Weight | Industry | Weight
  [90:5:1.0] Electricity | 30.93 | Coal | 5.60
  [90:5:1.1] Refinery products | 22.57 | Iron ore | 4.91
  [90:5:1.2] Steel | 17.58 | Cement | 4.41
  [90:5:1.3] Crude oil | 7.43 | Natural gas | 3.84
  [90:5:1.4] Fertilizers | 2.73 |  | 
[90:5:2] - **PAT → Carbon Credit Trading Scheme** for **9** sectors (cement, fertilisers, paper, textiles, petrochemicals …).
[90:5:3] ~~ Sources: app notes 4:90 sub 6, 4:88 sub 8

=== OWN MCQs of 4:90 (51)
[n9f294c14ceb] sub0
  Which one of the following is an agro-based industry?
  Iron and steel industry | Aluminium industry | Cement industry | *Cotton textile industry
  x: Cotton textiles are agro-based because cotton is an agricultural raw material. Cement, aluminium and iron and steel are mineral-based industries.
[n635748156c6] sub0
  Dairy is an example of which type of industry?
  Agro-based | Marine-based | *Animal-based | Forest-based
  x: Dairy is animal-based; sugar is agro-based, pulp and paper forest-based and coastal aquaculture processing marine-based.
[nworld-geography-32-13] sub0
  Which of the following is a forest-based industry?
  Petrochemicals | Oil refining | *Lac | Leather
  x: Lac is forest-based, like matchsticks, resin, bamboo products and rayon; petrochemicals and oil refining are fuel-based, leather animal-based.
[nde0ae9fa801] sub0
  Why do agro-based industrial units generally locate near the source of raw materials?
  gain weight after processing | *may perish or lose weight | are available everywhere | need cheap hydel power
  x: Agricultural raw materials may be perishable or lose weight in processing, so units locate near them.
[fa9899e5ad07b] sub0
  Coastal aquaculture processing is classified as which type of industry?
  *Marine-based | Agro-based | Mineral-based | Forest-based
  x: Industries based on sea produce, such as seafood and coastal aquaculture processing, are marine-based.
[f897ab420c733] sub0

  Furniture | Plywood | Matchsticks | *Pulp and paper
  x: Pulp and paper is the largest forest-based industry.
[fd74e01e2a26e] sub0
  On which raw material is rayon, or artificial silk, based?
  Raw silk | Jute fibre | Wool | *Wood pulp
  x: Rayon is made from wood pulp, so it is a forest-based industry.
[g9b92170b824f] sub0
  Which of the following is NOT a mineral-based industry?
  Cement | Copper smelting | Aluminium | *Plywood
  x: Plywood is made from timber, so it is forest-based; cement, copper smelting and aluminium use minerals.

  x: Leather and auto parts are associated with Nellore; Prakasam is associated with mineral-based industries.

[fde2e1de258c1] sub3
  Under the Industrial Policy Resolution 1956, for which sector were Schedule A industries such as arms and atomic energy reserved?
  *Public sector | Private sector | Cooperative sector | Joint sector
  x: Schedule A (arms, atomic energy, iron and steel) was for the public sector; Schedule B was open to both.
[n7d28ba4cd87] sub4
  The Ministry of Micro, Small and Medium Enterprises was formed in 2007 by merging which two ministries?
  Ministry of Rural Development and Ministry of Agriculture | Ministry of Finance and Ministry of Industries | Ministry of Commerce and Ministry of Heavy Industries | *Ministry of Small Scale Industries and Ministry of Agro and Rural Industries
  x: The Ministry of MSME was formed in 2007 by merging the Ministry of Small Scale Industries with the Ministry of Agro and Rural Industries.
[n6db699e3223] sub4
  The 2020 definition of MSMEs introduced which major change?
  Classification only by ownership | Classification exclusively by number of workers | Classification only by annual exports | *A composite criterion of investment and turnover, without distinguishing manufacturing from services
  x: The 2020 definition uses a composite criterion of investment and turnover and removes the earlier distinction between manufacturing and service enterprises.
[f9089a9782cdb] sub4
  From 1 April 2025, what is the investment limit of a micro enterprise?
  *₹2.5 crore | ₹10 crore | ₹25 crore | ₹1 crore
  x: Micro: investment up to ₹2.5 crore and turnover up to ₹10 crore.
[fd57125ed2fc3] sub4
  From 1 April 2025, what is the turnover ceiling of a medium enterprise?
  ₹100 crore | ₹250 crore | ₹125 crore | *₹500 crore
  x: Medium: investment up to ₹125 crore and turnover up to ₹500 crore; ₹100 crore is the small-enterprise turnover limit.
[g1a396663c5c2] sub4
  From 1 April 2025, what is the investment limit of a small enterprise?
  ₹2.5 crore | ₹10 crore | *₹25 crore | ₹125 crore
  x: A small enterprise may invest up to ₹25 crore with turnover up to ₹100 crore; ₹2.5 crore is the micro limit and ₹125 crore the medium limit.
[n9b0bc49a5e2] sub4
  In the use-based classification of industries, under which class do appliances fall?
  Capital goods | *Consumer durables | Intermediate goods | Consumer non-durables
  x: Appliances are consumer durables; machinery is capital goods, chemicals intermediate goods and food consumer non-durables.
[nea5dbddcdc0] sub4
  The term 'gold collar' is associated with which sector?
  *Quinary | Secondary | Quaternary | Primary
  x: The quinary sector, involving top-level decision-making, is called the 'gold collar' sector.
[n7434d15669e] sub4
  Which one of the following is classified as a basic or primary good?
  Soap | Television set | *Steel | Industrial tool
  x: Steel, cement and coal are examples of basic or primary goods.
[ned435cc0af0] sub4
  Which of the following is a sunset industry?
  Green hydrogen | Electric vehicles | *Legacy print | Semiconductors
  x: Legacy print and thermal coal equipment are sunset areas; green hydrogen, EVs, semiconductors and AI are sunrise areas.
[n2c5fe923bff] sub4
  Consider the following statements:
1. Heavy industries are always labour-intensive, while light industries are always capital-intensive.
2. Heavy industries use bulky inputs and produce bulky outputs.
Which of the statements given above is/are correct?
  1 only | *2 only | Both 1 and 2 | Neither 1 nor 2
  x: Heavy industries have bulky inputs and outputs and are generally capital-intensive; light industries use lighter inputs and make consumer goods, so statement 1 is wrong.
[n8019936e189] sub4
  How many use-based classes are employed in the Index of Industrial Production?
  Four | Five | Seven | *Six
  x: The IIP uses six use-based classes: primary, capital, intermediate, infrastructure/construction, consumer durables and consumer non-durables. There is no export goods class.
[f7c5e4b431d5d] sub4
  Diamond polishing and software are examples of which type of industry?
  *Footloose | Heavy | Agro-based | Mineral-based
  x: Footloose industries need skilled labour and internet, not a particular raw material.
[f281675e87c3e] sub4
  Consider the following statements about heavy and light industries:
1. Heavy industries are bulky and capital-intensive, such as HEC Ranchi.
2. Electronics assembly and garments are light industries.
Which of the statements given above is/are correct?
  1 only | 2 only | *Both 1 and 2 | Neither 1 nor 2
  x: Heavy industries such as iron and steel and HEC Ranchi are bulky and capital-intensive; light industries such as electronics assembly and garments use lighter inputs.
[g108c97ddbe18] sub4
  Which of the following is NOT a use-based class of the Index of Industrial Production (IIP)?
  Primary goods | Capital goods | *Export goods | Consumer durables
  x: The IIP has six use-based classes: primary, capital, intermediate, infrastructure/construction, consumer durables and consumer non-durables. There is no export goods class.
[nf027ae8d75d] sub5
  Which industry carries the highest weight in the revised Index of Core Industries (base 2022-23)?
  *Electricity | Cement | Coal | Steel
  x: Electricity has the highest weight (about 31%); the revised index has base 2022-23 and nine industries with iron ore added.
[nb4b91cd9457] sub5
  The Perform, Achieve and Trade scheme is moving to the Carbon Credit Trading Scheme for how many sectors?
  7 | 8 | *9 | 10
  x: Nine sectors, including cement, fertilisers, paper, textiles, petrochemicals and refining.
[fe01caba33ed6] sub5
  How many industries make up the revised Index of Core Industries (base 2022-23)?
  Eight | Ten | Seven | *Nine
  x: The revised index has nine industries after iron ore was added; electricity has the highest weight.
[n71f7bfd42f3] sub5
  The Index of Eight Core Industries (ICI) and the Index of Industrial Production (IIP) were both rebased in July of which year?
  2023 | 2024 | 2025 | *2026
  x: Both the ICI and the IIP were rebased in 2026, with the new series released in July 2026.
[f75d9a5e506b7] sub5
  In the Index of Core Industries rebased to 2022-23, which industry carries the highest weight?
  *Electricity | Refinery products | Steel | Crude oil
  x: Electricity has the heaviest weight (30.93), ahead of refinery products (22.57) and steel (17.58).
[fee8450ef315d] sub5
  After rebasing to 2022-23, how many industries does the Index of Core Industries cover?
  *9 | 8 | 10 | 7
  x: With iron ore added, the index now covers 9 industries, not 8.
[f29a5aeebea06] sub5
  Which industry was newly added to the Index of Core Industries in its 2022-23 base?
  Coal | Cement | Fertilizers | *Iron ore
  x: Iron ore is the newest member, taking the index from 8 to 9 industries.
[g0ac113c6643f] sub5
  Which of the following is NOT one of the industries in the Index of Core Industries?
  Iron ore | Fertilizers | Natural gas | *Textiles
  x: The nine core industries are electricity, refinery products, steel, crude oil, coal, iron ore, cement, natural gas and fertilizers; textiles is not one.
[g8febefff30ee] sub5
  In the Index of Core Industries (base 2022-23), which industry has the second-highest weight after electricity?
  Steel | *Refinery products | Coal | Crude oil
  x: Refinery products carry 22.57, next only to electricity (30.93); steel is third at 17.58.

=== NOTES 4:92 | Transport in India – Roads, Railways, Pipelines, Waterways and Airways
## SUB 0 | Golden Quadrilateral and the N-S / E-W Corridors
[92:0:0] TABLE head: Corridor | Fact
  [92:0:0.0] Golden Quadrilateral (GQ) | National Highway project linking Delhi–Mumbai–Chennai–Kolkata; ~5,846 km, 4/6 lanes; NHDP Phase I (NHDP launched 1998)
  [92:0:0.1] North-South | Srinagar–Kanyakumari (with Kochi–Salem spur), ~4,076 km; NHDP Phase I-II
  [92:0:0.2] East-West | Silchar (Assam)–Porbandar (Gujarat), ~3,640 km; 7 States
[92:0:1] - GQ = **highways**, not rail lines, air routes or power grids. **Hyderabad and Bengaluru are not GQ nodes.**
[92:0:2] - **Longest GQ arm: Kolkata–Chennai** (~1,649 km, NH-16). **Longest GQ stretch in one State: AP, 1,014 km.**
[92:0:3] - N-S and E-W corridors cross at **Jhansi** (UP) – not Nagpur, Gwalior or Bhopal.
[92:0:4] - N-S corridor north to south: **Srinagar – Agra – Gwalior – Jhansi – Nagpur – Hyderabad – Bengaluru – Krishnagiri – Kanyakumari** (NH-44, so it **passes through Hyderabad**).
[92:0:5] - Not on the corridors: Lucknow, Kanpur, Kota (Agra is on the N-S corridor).
[92:0:6] - UPSC 2023: E-W is not Dibrugarh–Surat; the Trilateral Highway is **Moreh–Mae Sot** (not Chiang Mai); BCIM is **Kunming–Kolkata** (not Varanasi) – **none** correct.
[92:0:7] - GQ 2.0 high-speed expressway network planned (Oct 2025).
[92:0:8] > Exam angle: Jhansi junction (asked 7 times), Silchar–Porbandar (5), Hyderabad not on GQ (3), Kolkata–Chennai longest arm (3).
[92:0:9] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Geography APPSC 11 p.3 (GQ, longest arm Kolkata–Chennai 1649 km, AP 1014 km, Jhansi junction); PMF Human & Economic Geography 02 p.6-7, p.22 (GQ 5,846 km, N-S 4,076 km, E-W 3,640 km, meet at Jhansi; UPSC 2023); OnlyIAS NCERT Human Geography 08 p.15
## SUB 1 | Trains, Firsts and Railway Technology
[92:1:0] TABLE head: Train / system | Fact
  [92:1:0.0] Vande Bharat Express | Earlier Train 18; India's first indigenous, engine-less semi-high-speed EMU; built by ICF Chennai; first run New Delhi–Varanasi (15 Feb 2019); operating 160 km/h (design 180 km/h), not 250 km/h
  [92:1:0.1] Vande Bharat Sleeper | First: Guwahati–Kolkata (2026)
  [92:1:0.2] Gatimaan Express | 2016, Delhi–Agra, 160 km/h – India's fastest train when asked; since 2018 runs on to Jhansi
  [92:1:0.3] Tejas Express | First run Mumbai–Karmali (Goa), 2017
  [92:1:0.4] Kisan Rail | First: Devlali (Nashik district, Maharashtra) – Danapur (Bihar), 7 Aug 2020
  [92:1:0.5] Bullet train | Mumbai–Ahmedabad high-speed rail (Japan)
  [92:1:0.6] Trivandrum Rajdhani | The longest-running Rajdhani (~3,100 km from Delhi)
  [92:1:0.7] First CNG train | Rewari–Rohtak (Haryana), 2015
  [92:1:0.8] First hydrogen train | Jind–Sonipat (Haryana)
  [92:1:0.9] First Green Rail Corridor | Rameswaram–Manamadurai, Tamil Nadu (2016, toilet-discharge-free)
  [92:1:0.10] Sky Bus | Konkan Railway's elevated Sky Bus; bogies by ICF Perambur
[92:1:1] - **Kavach** = indigenous **Automatic Train Protection** system: auto-brakes if the loco pilot fails, helps in bad weather; adopted as **National ATP in July 2020** (not June 2013).
[92:1:2] - **Diamond Quadrilateral** = **high-speed rail network** linking Delhi, Mumbai, Kolkata, Chennai (2014; NHSRCL).
[92:1:3] - **Mission 41K:** save **₹41,000 crore** in energy costs over a decade.
[92:1:4] - **Rakesh Mohan** Expert Group (2001) first recommended **corporatisation** of the Railways (Bibek Debroy 2015 came later).
[92:1:5] - Deutsche Bahn (Germany) = world's first **intermodal partner of Star Alliance** (Aug 2022).
[92:1:6] - Amrit Bharat Station Scheme: 1,324 stations.
[92:1:7] > Exam angle: Vande Bharat = Train 18, ICF, Delhi–Varanasi (asked 9 times).
[92:1:8] ~~ Sources: Praveen CA360 May 2026 magazine p.63 (Vande Bharat launched 2019, ICF Chennai); Rocket Indian Geography p.16 (first CNG train Rewari–Rohtak); PMF Human & Economic Geography 02 p.28 (Diamond Quadrilateral); app notes 4:92 (sub 4); other train facts: general knowledge, not checked
[92:1:9] - **1973:** a long-distance service from **Mangalore to Delhi** was then India's longest rail service.
## SUB 2 | National Highways – Numbering, Routes and Lengths
[92:2:0] - **Numbering (2010):** **odd** = north-south (rising **west to east**); **even** = east-west (rising **north to south**).
[92:2:1] TABLE head: NH (new) | Route | Old number / note
  [92:2:1.0] NH-44 | Srinagar – Kanyakumari | Longest NH: ~3,745 km in NCERT (later MoRTH ~4,112 km); 11 States; mostly old NH-7 + NH-2/1
  [92:2:1.1] NH-48 | Delhi – Jaipur – Mumbai – Bengaluru – Chennai | Old NH-8 + NH-4; GQ arm
  [92:2:1.2] NH-19 | Delhi – Kolkata | Old NH-2; GQ arm
  [92:2:1.3] NH-16 | Kolkata – Chennai, east coast | Old NH-5; GQ arm through coastal AP
  [92:2:1.4] NH-27 | Porbandar – Silchar | E-W corridor
  [92:2:1.5] NH-966B | Kundannoor – Willingdon Island, Kochi | Shortest, 8 km
[92:2:2] - **Old NH-1:** **Delhi–Amritsar via Ambala and Jalandhar** (now part of NH-44); Delhi–Kolkata was **old NH-2**.
[92:2:3] - **Old NH-7** (Varanasi–Kanyakumari via Jabalpur, Nagpur, Hyderabad, Bengaluru, Madurai) was the **longest NH** before 2010; it did **not pass Pune**.
[92:2:4] - **NH-44** passes Delhi, Agra, Jhansi, Nagpur, Hyderabad, Bengaluru (UP, Telangana, AP, TN) – **not Pune** (NH-48/NH-65) and **not Kerala**.
[92:2:5] - **Old NH-4** = Mumbai (Thane)–Pune–Bengaluru–Chittoor–Chennai: Maharashtra, Karnataka, AP, TN – not UP, MP or Rajasthan.
[92:2:6] - Old routes by length: **Kolkata–Hazira (NH-6, 1,949 km)** > Chennai–Thane (NH-4) > Agra–Mumbai (NH-3) > Pune–Machilipatnam (NH-9).
[92:2:7] - UPSC 2014 (new numbers): NH-4 is in the Andamans, NH-6 Assam–Mizoram, NH-15 Assam–Arunachal – none of the pairs correct.
[92:2:8] - **Longest NH length by State: Maharashtra** (17,756.6 km); Maharashtra also has the longest total road length.
[92:2:9] > Exam angle: "longest NH" = NH-44 now, NH-7 in pre-2010 papers.
[92:2:10] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Geography APPSC 11 p.1-3 (old/new NH numbers; Maharashtra 17,756.6 km; NH-44 longest, 11 States; NH-966B shortest); OnlyIAS NCERT Human Geography 08 p.13 (NH-44 ~3,745 km); PMF Human & Economic Geography 02 p.20-23 (UPSC 2014, UPSC 2004 Kolkata–Hazira); app notes 4:92 (sub 0)
## SUB 3 | Railways – History, Network, Konkan and Units
[92:3:0] - **First train: 16 April 1853**, **Bori Bunder (Bombay) – Thane, 34 km**; by **GIPR**, under **Lord Dalhousie**.
[92:3:1] - **Broad gauge = 1.676 m (5½ ft)**; metre 1 m; narrow 0.762 / 0.610 m. BG >95% of the network.
[92:3:2] - Network **70,000+ route km**; **4th largest** in the world (after USA, Russia, China); freight **1,617 MT (2024-25)** = world's **2nd-largest freight railway**.
[92:3:3] - **Coal** is the largest single commodity carried (about half of freight).
[92:3:4] - Railway Budget 2012-13: about **13.6 lakh employees** (13,61,519).
[92:3:5] - **Konkan Railway:** **760 km, Roha (Maharashtra) – Mangalore (Thokur, Karnataka)**, opened **1998**; partners **Maharashtra, Goa, Karnataka** (Kerala benefits most as a gateway); runs through Ratnagiri, Madgaon, Udupi – **not Belgaum** and **not Kerala**; KRCL HQ **Navi Mumbai**; a **PSU, not a zone**.
[92:3:6] - First rail track on **PPP**: **Gujarat** (Pipavav port line).
[92:3:7] TABLE head: Unit | Location
  [92:3:7.0] CLW | Chittaranjan, West Bengal
  [92:3:7.1] BLW (earlier Diesel Locomotive Works) | Varanasi
  [92:3:7.2] ICF | Perambur, Chennai (1955, Swiss collaboration) – Tamil Nadu
  [92:3:7.3] RCF | Kapurthala, Punjab (1986)
  [92:3:7.4] MCF | Rae Bareli, UP
  [92:3:7.5] Wheel and Axle Plant | Bengaluru
  [92:3:7.6] CORE (electrification) | Prayagraj (Allahabad) – not Varanasi
  [92:3:7.7] Railway Staff College (now NAIR) | Vadodara
  [92:3:7.8] NRTI (now Gati Shakti Vishwavidyalaya) | Vadodara
  [92:3:7.9] Railway museum (Aug 2020) | Hubballi
[92:3:8] - **UNESCO Mountain Railways of India:** **Darjeeling Himalayan (West Bengal; Siliguri–Darjeeling)**, Nilgiri Mountain (TN), Kalka–Shimla (HP).
[92:3:9] - **Highest railway station: Ghum (West Bengal)**, ~2,258 m, on the Darjeeling line.
[92:3:10] - **NE capitals on rail:** in 2015 only **Agartala**; Itanagar (Naharlagun) and Aizawl (Sairang, 2025) joined later.
[92:3:11] - Longest platform: **Hubballi** (1,507 m) now – Gorakhpur held the record when asked (SSC 2012).
[92:3:12] > Exam angle: production unit–location matches; 1853 Bombay–Thane; Konkan route and partners.
[92:3:13] ~~ Sources: OnlyIAS NCERT Human Geography 08 p.16-17 (1853 Bombay–Thane 34 km; Konkan 760 km Roha–Mangalore, Maharashtra, Goa, Karnataka; gauges 1.676 m); PMF Human & Economic Geography 02 p.7, p.27-30 (4th largest; Konkan; UPSC 1998 Konkan answer); app notes 4:92 (sub 2, sub 5); other entries: general knowledge, not checked
[92:3:14] - **Longest station name:** Venkatanarasimharajuvaripeta (AP); **Chennai Central** (renamed Puratchi Thalaivar Dr M.G. Ramachandran Central) is said to miss it by one letter.

## SUB 5 | Tunnels, Bridges and Records
[92:5:0] TABLE head: Structure | Location | Fact
  [92:5:0.0] Atal Tunnel (BRO) | Under Rohtang Pass, Pir Panjal, HP | Manali – Lahaul-Spiti; 9.02 km; world's longest highway tunnel above 10,000 ft (not the world's longest)
  [92:5:0.1] Chenani–Nashri (Dr S.P. Mookerjee) | J&K, NH-44 | 9.28 km – India's longest road tunnel
  [92:5:0.2] Pamban Bridge (1914) | Palk Strait, TN | India's first sea bridge
  [92:5:0.3] New Pamban Bridge | Mandapam – Rameswaram | 2.08 km; India's first vertical-lift railway sea bridge (2025); built by RVNL (Navratna)
  [92:5:0.4] Bhupen Hazarika (Dhola-Sadiya) Setu | Lohit river, Assam | Assam – Arunachal; 9.15 km (2017)
  [92:5:0.5] Chenab Bridge | Reasi, J&K (USBRL) | 359 m; world's highest railway arch bridge
  [92:5:0.6] Anji Khad | J&K (USBRL) | India's first cable-stayed rail bridge
  [92:5:0.7] Godavari Arch Bridge | Rajahmundry | Spans two channels, so also called the Kovvur–Rajahmundry Bridge; not the latest Godavari bridge
  [92:5:0.8] Maitri Setu | Feni river | Sabroom (Tripura) – Ramgarh (Bangladesh)
  [92:5:0.9] Zojila Tunnel | Zojila pass | Sonamarg – Drass/Kargil
  [92:5:0.10] Janasu Tunnel | Rishikesh–Karnaprayag | 14.58 km; breakthrough Apr 2025; longest transport tunnel
[92:5:1] - **USBRL:** 272 km; 36 tunnels, 943 bridges; **T-50** = India's longest rail tunnel. **Pir Panjal** rail tunnel (~11 km) > Karbude > Nathuwadi > Berdewadi (Konkan).
[92:5:2] - **Highest motorable road:** built by **BRO** in Ladakh, ~**19,000 ft** (Umling La / Mig La).
[92:5:3] TABLE head: World record | Holder

  [92:5:3.5] Highest civilian airport | Daocheng Yading, China
  [92:5:3.6] Longest platform (now) | Shree Hubballi Siddharoodha Swamiji station, Karnataka (1,507 m)

## SUB 6 | Roads – Network, Traffic, BRO and Expressways
[92:6:0] - Road network **63.45 lakh km**, **2nd largest** in the world; NHs **1.46 lakh km**.
[92:6:1] - NHs = about **2%** of road length but carry about **40%** of road traffic; roads carry ~85% of passengers and 70% of freight.
[92:6:2] - **Road density:** highest in **Kerala**.
[92:6:3] - **Surface roads per 100 km² (UPSC 1998):** Punjab and Tamil Nadu far ahead of Maharashtra and Haryana (books differ on which of the two leads).
[92:6:4] - Road transport got the post-1947 impetus because rail is capital-heavy and indivisible and cannot reach scattered settlements – but **rail is cheaper per tonne-km**.
[92:6:5] - **BRO:** set up **1960** (Ministry of Defence); builds border roads.
[92:6:6] - **Mumbai–Pune Expressway** (94.5 km) = India's **first six-lane concrete expressway**; **Agra–Lucknow** six-lane expressway opened Nov 2016 (UP).
[92:6:7] - **Bharatmala Pariyojana (2017):** ~**34,800 km** in Phase I; highest built length **Rajasthan** (2,241 km to Oct 2024).
[92:6:8] - **Madhya Pradesh** first to issue a unified vehicle registration card.
[92:6:9] - World: **Autobahn** = Germany's highways (Autoroute = France); **Asian Highway** promoted by **UNESCAP**; the Silk Route was a land-and-sea network, not one modern highway.
[92:6:10] ~~ Sources: OnlyIAS NCERT Human Geography 08 p.13-14 (2% length, 40% traffic; BRO May 1960); PMF Human & Economic Geography 02 p.24-26 (BRO 1960; road density Kerala; Bharatmala 34,800 km; UPSC 1998 road density and expressway answers); Rocket Indian Geography p.16 (first six-lane concrete expressway Mumbai–Pune); app notes 4:92 (sub 0, sub 1)
## SUB 7 | National Waterways
[92:7:0] - **IWAI:** set up **1986**, HQ **Noida**. **National Waterways Act 2016:** **111 NWs**. NINI (training) is at **Patna**.
[92:7:1] TABLE head: NW | Stretch | States | Length
  [92:7:1.0] NW-1 | Ganga-Bhagirathi-Hooghly; Haldia–Prayagraj (Allahabad) | UP, Bihar, Jharkhand, WB | 1,620 km
  [92:7:1.1] NW-2 | Brahmaputra; Sadiya–Dhubri | Assam | 891 km
  [92:7:1.2] NW-3 | West Coast (Kerala coastal) Canal; Kottapuram–Kollam | Kerala | 205 km
  [92:7:1.3] NW-4 | Kakinada–Puducherry canals + Godavari and Krishna | AP, TN | 1,095 km as first declared (~2,890 km after the 2016 extension)
  [92:7:1.4] NW-5 | Brahmani–Mahanadi delta, East Coast Canal | Odisha, WB | 588 km
[92:7:2] - NW-4 is the waterway on the **Krishna** in AP; its Godavari stretch (Bhadrachalam/Cherla–Rajahmundry) is declared too.
[92:7:3] - **Longest NW** in exam keys: **NW-1 (Allahabad–Haldia)**.
[92:7:4] - **Waterways = cheapest mode** for long-distance bulky goods.
[92:7:5] - **Jal Marg Vikas** (**World Bank**): Varanasi–Haldia on NW-1. **Jalvahak** (2024): NW-1, 2, 16. **Budget 2026-27:** 20 new NWs, beginning with NW-5.
[92:7:6] - IWT cargo 146 MMT (2024-25); target **200 MMT by 2030**.
[92:7:7] ~~ Sources: PMF Human & Economic Geography 02 p.31-33 (111 NWs; IWAI Noida; NW table with NW-4 2,890 km); OnlyIAS NCERT Human Geography 08 p.20 (water transport cheapest); app notes 4:92 (sub 7); NINI Patna: general knowledge, not checked
## SUB 8 | Pipelines
[92:8:0] TABLE head: Pipeline | Route | Point
  [92:8:0.0] Naharkatia–Nunmati–Barauni | Assam → Bihar | First crude pipeline in India; Asia's first cross-country pipeline (OIL, 1,157 km; extended to Kanpur 1966)
  [92:8:0.1] HBJ / HVJ | Hazira (Gujarat) – Bijaipur (MP) – Jagdishpur (UP) | ~1,750 km gas pipeline built by GAIL
  [92:8:0.2] Salaya–Koyali–Mathura | Salaya (Gulf of Kutch) – Mathura – Jalandhar | Crude
  [92:8:0.3] Kandla–Bathinda | Gujarat – Punjab | Crude
  [92:8:0.4] Visakhapatnam–Secunderabad | AP – Telangana | ~600 km pipeline (APPSC 2002 key: GAIL)
  [92:8:0.5] PM Urja Ganga (GAIL) | Jagdishpur–Haldia, Bokaro–Dhamra | Gas pipeline, foundation 24 Oct 2016 (Varanasi); not from Iran
[92:8:1] - HBJ fertiliser plants: Bijaipur, Sawai Madhopur, Jagdishpur, Shahjahanpur, Aonla, Babrala (**not Ramagundam**).
[92:8:2] - **Pipelines = cheapest for oil and gas**.

## SUB 9 | Airways
[92:9:0] - **First airmail:** **18 Feb 1911**, **Allahabad – Naini**.
[92:9:1] - **RCS-UDAN** under the **National Civil Aviation Policy 2016**; NCAP 2016 offered **"Open Skies"** – signed by Sri Lanka, Spain, Jamaica and others, **not Nepal**. Modified UDAN: 120 destinations, **4 crore** passengers in 10 years.
[92:9:2] - **DigiYatra** began **1 Dec 2022** at **Delhi, Bengaluru, Varanasi** (not Mumbai).
[92:9:3] - **Pakyong Airport = Sikkim** (first airport of the State).
[92:9:4] - **Panna** has no operational airport (Khajuraho, Jabalpur, Gwalior do).
[92:9:5] - Greenfield Airports Policy **2008**; Open Sky for **six metro airports**; **Bharatiya Vayuyan Adhiniyam 2024** replaced the Aircraft Act 1934 from **Jan 2025**; Budget 2026-27 **Seaplane VGF**.
[92:9:6] - **Delhi** = first carbon-neutral airport in Asia-Pacific.
[92:9:7] ~~ Sources: PMF Human & Economic Geography 02 p.36 (airmail 1911 Allahabad–Naini); Rocket CA Feb 2026 p.66 (Pakyong Airport, Sikkim); app notes 4:92 (sub 8); DigiYatra and Open Skies: general knowledge, not checked
## SUB 10 | Electrification and Freight Corridors
[92:10:0] - **Electrification:** about **94%** of BG as on 1 Mar 2024 (PYQ key) – now **99.6%** (23 May 2026); 14 of 18 zones at 100%.
[92:10:1] - India = **2nd largest** electrified network (after **China**). **Haryana** = first fully electrified State.

  [92:10:2.3] New (Budget 2026-27) | Dankuni – Surat | –
[92:10:3] - **National Rail Plan 2030:** rail freight share **28% → 45%**.
[92:10:4] - **Budget 2026-27:** **7 high-speed rail corridors**.

## SUB 11 | Gati Shakti, Logistics and Modes Compared
[92:11:0] - **PM Gati Shakti (2021)** = GIS-based **National Master Plan for multimodal connectivity**; **16 ministries** on one platform; apex Empowered Group of Secretaries under the **Cabinet Secretary**.
[92:11:1] - **National Logistics Policy 2022** complements it; **LEADS** (logistics index of States) since 2018; LPI rank **44 (2018) → 38 (2023)**.
[92:11:2] TABLE head: Mode | Feature
  [92:11:2.0] Inland waterways | Cheapest freight; fuel per tonne-km ~1/3 of rail, 1/7 of road
  [92:11:2.1] Shipping | 95% of trade by volume, 68% by value
  [92:11:2.2] Pipelines | Cheapest for oil and gas
  [92:11:2.3] Rail | Cheaper per tonne-km than road (~half); ~90% less CO2
  [92:11:2.4] Road | Door-to-door; short distance – not the cheapest
  [92:11:2.5] Air | Fastest and costliest
[92:11:3] ~~ Sources: app notes 4:92 (sub 9, sub 10); OnlyIAS NCERT Human Geography 08 p.20 (water transport cheapest)
