=== NOTES 4:93 | Transport in India – Waterways, Shipping, Ports and Civil Aviation
## SUB 0 | Civil Aviation – History, Bodies and Firsts
[93:0:0] # Milestones
[93:0:1] TABLE head: Year | Event
  [93:0:1.0] 1911 | First flight: airmail Allahabad (Prayagraj) – Naini, about 10 km, 18 Feb 1911
  [93:0:1.1] 1953 | Air Corporations Act (assent 28 May 1953): air transport nationalised → Indian Airlines (domestic) + Air India International
  [93:0:1.2] 1995 | Airports Authority of India (AAI) formed; manages most Indian airports
  [93:0:1.3] 1999 | Cochin (CIAL) – first airport owned by a public limited company (PPP)
  [93:0:1.4] 2012 | Kingfisher Airlines stopped flying (licence suspended, Oct 2012)
  [93:0:1.5] 2016 | UDAN (Ude Desh ka Aam Nagrik) launched – works through viability gap funding
  [93:0:1.6] 2017 | India's first integrated heliport: Rohini, Delhi (Pawan Hans)
  [93:0:1.7] 24 Jun 2020 | Kushinagar (UP) declared an international airport
[93:0:2] - **1953 nationalisation:** **9 airline companies** in most keys – **8 domestic** carriers merged into Indian Airlines + Air-India International.
[93:0:3] - **UDAN fare cap:** about half the seats on a ~1-hour (~500 km) flight capped at **Rs 2,500**.
[93:0:4] - **Training:** **Civil Aviation Training Centre – Allahabad (Bamrauli)**; **Indira Gandhi Rashtriya Uran Akademi – Fursatganj, Raebareli (UP)**.
[93:0:5] - **Aakhiri Ahuti (Jan 2017):** **IndiGo** + Delhi Police fly **mortal remains** of needy **North-East** people free of cost.
[93:0:6] - **Airlines:** **KLM** (Netherlands, 1919) = oldest airline still under its original name; **Lufthansa = Germany**.
[93:0:7] - **Mayday** = international radio **distress call** (life-threatening emergency) at sea or in the air.
[93:0:8] - **IC-814 hijack (Dec 1999):** Kathmandu → **Amritsar → Lahore → Dubai → Kandahar**.
[93:0:9] > Exam angle: "1911 / Allahabad-Naini", "1953 / Air Corporations Act", "Cochin = public limited company" are the repeats.
[93:0:10] ~~ Sources: PMF Human & Economic Geography 02 p36 (1911 Allahabad-Naini); app notes 4:93 (sub 7); orfonline.org (nine airlines, 1953); business-standard.com (Aakhiri Ahuti, IndiGo); heliport, training centres, KLM, UDAN cap: general knowledge, not checked
## SUB 1 | Airports and Aviation Market
[93:1:0] - **Operational airports:** **74 (2014) → 164 (2025)**; PMF: **149** operational in 2023 with **33 international** (older exam key: **16** international airports).
[93:1:1] - India = **3rd largest domestic** aviation market.
[93:1:2] - **Busiest airport:** **Delhi IGI** (~78.1 million passengers in 2025; 11th in world); Mumbai 2nd (~55.5 million).
[93:1:3] - **Cochin (CIAL)** = world's **first fully solar-powered airport**.
[93:1:4] - **Veer Savarkar International Airport = Port Blair** (Sri Vijaya Puram), Andaman and Nicobar.
[93:1:5] - **Highest airports:** **Leh** = India's highest commercial airport; world's highest = **Daocheng Yading, China** (~4,411 m); **Lhasa Gonggar ~3,570 m** – the answer when Kathmandu, Tehran, Tashkent are the other options.
[93:1:6] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Geography 11 p8 (airport list: Veer Savarkar – Port Blair); PMF Human & Economic Geography 02 p36 (149 airports, 33 international, 2023); app notes 4:93 (sub 7)
## SUB 2 | Major Ports – Types and Locations
[93:2:0] TABLE head: Port | State | Type / feature
  [93:2:0.0] Deendayal (Kandla) | Gujarat | Tidal port at the eastern end of the Gulf of Kachchh (Kutch); free trade zone
  [93:2:0.1] Mumbai | Maharashtra | Natural harbour (Salsette Island); biggest port, about 1/5 of traffic
  [93:2:0.2] JNPA (Nhava Sheva) | Maharashtra | Built to relieve Mumbai; largest container port
  [93:2:0.3] Mormugao (Marmugao) | Goa | Natural port at the Zuari estuary; major port 1963
  [93:2:0.4] New Mangalore | Karnataka | Artificial lagoon port; Kudremukh iron ore
  [93:2:0.5] Kochi | Kerala | Natural harbour (Vembanad)
  [93:2:0.6] VOC Tuticorin | Tamil Nadu | Artificial deep-sea harbour
  [93:2:0.7] Chennai | Tamil Nadu | Oldest (and largest) artificial port
  [93:2:0.8] Kamarajar (Ennore) | Tamil Nadu | 12th major port; first corporatised port
  [93:2:0.9] Visakhapatnam | Andhra Pradesh | Deepest land-locked and protected port; "Brightest Jewel"
  [93:2:0.10] Paradip | Odisha | Deep-water port, Mahanadi delta; 8th major port (1966)
  [93:2:0.11] Kolkata-Haldia (Syama Prasad Mookerjee) | West Bengal | Oldest major port; only riverine port (Hooghly)
[93:2:1] - **Natural (sheltered) harbours on the west coast:** **Mumbai, Mormugao, Kochi**; east coast ports mostly **artificial**.
[93:2:2] - **Three major ports each:** **Tamil Nadu** (Chennai, Kamarajar, VOC) – the CSD 2018 key; **Maharashtra** gets a third with **Vadhavan**.
[93:2:3] - **13th major port: Vadhavan (Palghar, Maharashtra)**, approved 2020; older books call **Port Blair** the 13th (2010).
[93:2:4] - **7** major ports on the west coast (incl. Vadhavan) vs **6** on the east coast. **Visakhapatnam = AP's only major port.**
[93:2:5] - **West coast, south → north:** Thiruvananthapuram → Kochi → Kozhikode (Calicut) → Mangaluru.
[93:2:6] - **Shortest sea route** among exam options: **Chennai – Port Blair** (~1,190 km).
[93:2:7] > Match angle (SI 2016): Kandla – tidal, Chennai – artificial, Mumbai – biggest, Kolkata – riverine.
[93:2:8] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Geography 11 p6-7 (book p.146-147); NextGen Rocket Sheets Indian Geography (largest artificial – Chennai; largest natural – Mumbai; Kandla – tidal + Gulf of Kutch); app notes 4:93 (sub 0, 1); sea route, west-coast order: general knowledge, not checked
## SUB 3 | Major vs Non-major Ports; East and West Coasts
[93:3:0] TABLE head: Point | Major ports | Non-major ports
  [93:3:0.0] Entry | Union List Entry 27 | Concurrent List Entry 31
  [93:3:0.1] Law / run by | Major Port Authorities Act 2021; Union | State Maritime Boards
[93:3:1] - Shipping carries about **95%** of India's trade by **volume** and **68%** by **value** (2026).
[93:3:2] - **East coast has fewer ports:** **shallow continental shelf** + **cyclones and storms** (APPSC 2023 key: statements 1 and 2).
[93:3:3] - **Deep water close to shore** is found on the **west coast**, not the east; west = sheltered natural harbours, east = mostly artificial.
[93:3:4] ~~ Sources: app notes 4:93 (sub 0-2)
## SUB 4 | Port Traffic and Rankings
[93:4:0] - Major ports: record **915.17 million tonnes** in **2025-26**, up **7.06%**; beat **904 mt** target (6 Apr 2026).
[93:4:1] - **Largest cargo-handling major port: Paradip** (2026); it overtook **Deendayal**, which was the APPSC 2019 key (Rocket: Deendayal/Kandla).
[93:4:2] - **Largest container port: JNPA.** Largest privately owned port: **Mundra**.
[93:4:3] TABLE head: LPPI 2024-25 | Leader
  [93:4:3.0] Container | Mundra
  [93:4:3.1] Dry bulk | Paradip
  [93:4:3.2] Liquid bulk | Sikka
[93:4:4] - World Bank **CPPI 2024**: 2 Indian ports in top 30, 7 in top 100; turnaround ~30 hours.
[93:4:5] ~~ Sources: app notes 4:93 (sub 3); NextGen Rocket Sheets Indian Geography (largest major port by cargo – Deendayal; Mundra largest private)
## SUB 5 | National Waterways and Inland Water Transport
[93:5:0] - **Navigable inland waterways: about 14,500 km** (rivers, canals, backwaters, creeks) – about **1%** of transport.
[93:5:1] - **IWAI:** IWAI Act **1985**, set up **1986**, HQ **Noida**. **CIWTC** (Central Inland Water Transport Corporation) HQ **Kolkata**.
[93:5:2] - **National Waterways Act 2016** declared **111 NWs** (5 existing + 106 new); 25 viable, 13 operational. Before late 2008 only **3 NWs** existed (CDS 2008: statement "three NWs" was then true).
[93:5:3] TABLE head: NW | Route | Length
  [93:5:3.0] NW-1 (1986) | Ganga-Bhagirathi-Hooghly, Allahabad (Prayagraj) – Haldia | 1,620 km
  [93:5:3.1] NW-2 | Brahmaputra, Sadiya-Dhubri (Assam) | 891 km
  [93:5:3.2] NW-3 | West Coast Canal, Kollam-Kottapuram (Kerala) | 205 km
  [93:5:3.3] NW-4 | Kakinada-Puducherry canals (incl. Buckingham Canal) + Godavari, Krishna (AP, TN) | 1,095 km (old); 2,890 km after 2016
  [93:5:3.4] NW-5 | Talcher-Dhamra, Brahmani-Mahanadi delta + East Coast Canal (Odisha, WB) | 588 km
[93:5:4] - **Second longest NW (old figures): NW-4 / Buckingham Canal** after NW-1; NW-2 Sadiya-Dhubri is **not** the longest.
[93:5:5] - NW-1 terminals include **Haldia, Kolkata, Varanasi, Prayagraj**; NW-2 terminals include **Dhubri, Jogighopa, Tezpur, Dibrugarh, Sadiya**.
[93:5:6] - **Coastal shipping** grew **118%** in a decade.
[93:5:7] ~~ Sources: PMF Human & Economic Geography 02 p30-33; OnlyIAS NCERT Human Geography 09 p1 (14,500 km); NextGen AP Geography 03 p17 (NW-4 1,095 km); app notes 4:93 (sub 4); CIWTC Kolkata: general knowledge, not checked
## SUB 6 | Shipping – Schemes, Bodies and Heritage
[93:6:0] TABLE head: Scheme / law | Year | Provision
  [93:6:0.0] Sagarmala | 2015, MoPSW | Port-led development; 800+ projects (~₹5.8 lakh crore)
  [93:6:0.1] Ministry renamed MoPSW | 2020 | Ports, Shipping and Waterways
  [93:6:0.2] Maritime India Vision 2030 | 2021 | 150+ initiatives; India among top 10 maritime nations
  [93:6:0.3] Sagarmala 2.0 | 2025 | Shipbuilding, ship repair, ship breaking and recycling
  [93:6:0.4] 2025 laws | 2025 | Merchant Shipping Act, Coastal Shipping Act, Indian Ports Act
  [93:6:0.5] Maritime Amrit Kaal Vision 2047 | - | MIV 2030 feeds into it; 10 billion tonnes per annum capacity
[93:6:1] # Sagarmala – five pillars
[93:6:2] - Port modernisation; port connectivity; port-led industrialisation; coastal community development; coastal shipping + inland water transport.
[93:6:3] - **Nationalisation of private shipping companies** is NOT a Sagarmala pillar.
[93:6:4] # Bodies and places
[93:6:5] - **National Maritime Heritage Complex:** MoPSW, at **Lothal, Gujarat** – **Asia's largest maritime heritage museum**.
[93:6:6] - **IMO** HQ: **London**; UN specialised agency; 176 members, 3 associate members.
[93:6:7] - **Alang-Sosiya** (Bhavnagar, **Gujarat**, Gulf of Khambhat) = largest ship-breaking yard.
[93:6:8] ~~ Sources: app notes 4:93 (sub 5); Lothal, Alang: general knowledge, not checked

=== NOTES 4:94 | Communication Networks in India – Postal, Telecom and Digital Connectivity
## SUB 0 | Postal Network – PIN, Speed Post and India Post
[94:0:0] - **PIN code:** **6 digits**, from **15 Aug 1972**. 1st = zone; 2nd = sub-zone; 3rd = sorting district; last 3 = delivery office.
[94:0:1] - **9 PIN zones:** **8 regional** (1-8) + **1 functional** zone **9 = Army Postal Service**.
[94:0:2] TABLE head: First digit | Region (exam examples)
  [94:0:2.0] 1 | Delhi, Haryana, Punjab, HP, J&K
  [94:0:2.1] 2 | Uttar Pradesh (Lucknow), Uttarakhand
  [94:0:2.2] 3 | Rajasthan, Gujarat (Gandhinagar)
  [94:0:2.3] 4 | Maharashtra (Nagpur), MP, Chhattisgarh, Goa
  [94:0:2.4] 5 | Andhra Pradesh (Amaravati), Telangana, Karnataka
  [94:0:2.5] 6 | Tamil Nadu, Kerala
  [94:0:2.6] 7 | West Bengal, Odisha, North-East
  [94:0:2.7] 8 | Bihar, Jharkhand
[94:0:3] - **Digi PIN:** **10-digit alphanumeric**, on the **APT** platform built by **CEPT**; rollout in 23 circles done **4 Aug 2025** (1.64 lakh offices).
[94:0:4] - **Speed Post: Aug 1986** (to compete with couriers), refund if late. **IPPB: 1 Sep 2018**, Department of Posts.
[94:0:5] - **Project Arrow (2008):** India Post's programme to upgrade post offices and postal services.
[94:0:6] - India has the **largest number of post offices** in the world: **1,64,999** (31 Mar 2025). **AP: 10,660**.
[94:0:7] > Match angle (SI 2018): Lucknow 2, Gandhinagar 3, Nagpur 4, Amaravati 5.
[94:0:8] ~~ Sources: app notes 4:94 (sub 1, 8); PIN zone digits, Project Arrow: general knowledge, not checked
## SUB 1 | Mass Media – Radio, Television and Development Communication
[94:1:0] TABLE head: Year | Event
  [94:1:0.0] 1927 | Indian Broadcasting Company (private)
  [94:1:0.1] 1930 | Indian State Broadcasting Service (the "Indian Broadcasting Service" of exam options)
  [94:1:0.2] 1936 | Renamed All India Radio
  [94:1:0.3] 1957 | AIR named Akashvani
  [94:1:0.4] 15 Sep 1959 | Doordarshan begins in Delhi
  [94:1:0.5] 1991 / 1992 / 1995 | Private TV: Star (1991) → Zee (1992) → Sony (1995)
[94:1:1] - **Traditional TV broadcasting = terrestrial** (over-the-air) transmission; cable, DTH and IPTV came later.
[94:1:2] - **Prasar Bharati** (Act 1990) runs AIR and Doordarshan; **Yojana** magazine = Ministry of **Information & Broadcasting** (Publications Division).
[94:1:3] - First community radio: **Anna FM** (Anna University, **2004**); licensed by MIB. Community radio = participatory tool of development communication.
[94:1:4] - **Edgar Dale's Cone of Experience (1946):** direct experiences → … → **radio, recordings, still pictures** → visual symbols → **verbal symbols**; **television** was added only in **1954**.
[94:1:5] ~~ Sources: app notes 4:94 (sub 7); web: elearningindustry.com and cec.nic.in (Dale's cone, TV added 1954); Star/Zee/Sony dates, Yojana: general knowledge, not checked
## SUB 2 | Telecom – Regulators, Milestones and Market
[94:2:0] TABLE head: Body | Basis | Function
  [94:2:0.0] TRAI | TRAI Act 1997 | Regulator of telecom and broadcasting; tariffs; advises on spectrum, licensing
  [94:2:0.1] TDSAT | 2000 amendment of TRAI Act | Disputes; appeals against TRAI
  [94:2:0.2] DoT | Ministry of Communications | Policy, licensing, spectrum, digital connectivity, telecom R&D
  [94:2:0.3] Prasar Bharati | Act 1990 (Broadcasting Wing of MIB) | Broadcaster: runs AIR and Doordarshan (not DoT)
  [94:2:0.4] PRGI | Press and Registration of Periodicals Act 2023 (replaced PRB Act 1867) | Newspaper registration; earlier RNI (not Press Council)
[94:2:1] - Minister: **Jyotiraditya Scindia**; MoS: **Pemmasani Chandra Sekhar**. Fraud tools: **Sanchar Saathi + ASTR**.
[94:2:2] - **Fastest indigenous IP/MPLS router** launched in **Bengaluru** (Mar 2024).
[94:2:3] TABLE head: Year | Milestone
  [94:2:3.0] 1882 | First telephone exchange, Calcutta
  [94:2:3.1] 1994 / 1999 | Telecom Policies: liberalisation
  [94:2:3.2] 2011 / 3 Jul 2015 | MNP within circle / nationwide
  [94:2:3.3] 2018 | National Digital Communications Policy (5G, IoT, M2M)
  [94:2:3.4] Oct 2022 | 5G launched
  [94:2:3.5] 2023 | Telecommunications Act replaced Telegraph Act 1885
[94:2:4] - **Teledensity 86.76%** (2025-26; 75.23% in 2014). 5G: **99.9%** of districts, ~**40 crore** users in ~22 months.
[94:2:5] - **5G Standalone** = independent of 4G core; low latency, network slicing. **Bharat 6G Vision (2030)**.
[94:2:6] - India = **2nd largest telephone network** in the world (after China).
[94:2:7] - **Operators by subscribers:** early 2019 – **Vodafone Idea 1, Airtel 2, Jio 3, BSNL 4**; **now Jio 1, Airtel 2, Vi 3, BSNL 4**.
[94:2:8] ~~ Sources: app notes 4:94 (sub 0, 2); operator ranking, telephone-network rank: general knowledge, not checked
## SUB 3 | Transmission Media and Submarine Cables
[94:3:0] TABLE head: Medium | Signal | Feature
  [94:3:0.0] Optical fibre | Light by total internal reflection | Fastest, highest bandwidth (APPSC 2025)
  [94:3:0.1] Copper | Electrical | Low bandwidth, loss, interference
  [94:3:0.2] Satellite | Microwave | Remote reach; GEO ~36,000 km = high latency
[94:3:1] - Fibre **core index higher than cladding**; angle > critical angle. Uses: **endoscope** (one bundle lights, one carries image), **sensors** (temperature, strain), fibre lamps.
[94:3:2] TABLE head: Cable | Links | Inaugurated
  [94:3:2.0] CANI | Chennai - Andaman and Nicobar | 10 Aug 2020
  [94:3:2.1] KLI | Kochi - Lakshadweep | Jan 2024
[94:3:3] ~~ Sources: app notes 4:94 (sub 3, 4)
## SUB 4 | Digital Connectivity – BharatNet, Digital India, PM-WANI
[94:4:0] - **Digital India: 1 Jul 2015**, **9 pillars**, first = broadband highways.
[94:4:1] - **BharatNet:** ex-**NOFN (2011)** (not NKN) – optic fibre broadband to all **Gram Panchayats**; funded by **USOF (Digital Bharat Nidhi)**; nodal **BBNL** (merged with BSNL); **2.18 lakh GPs**, **6.92 lakh km** fibre; Phase 3 = last mile + private players. Amended BharatNet: **₹1.39 lakh crore**.
[94:4:2] - **PM-WANI: Dec 2020** (DoT); public Wi-Fi via **PDOs** and **PDOAs**. **National Broadband Mission: Dec 2019**.
[94:4:3] - **CSCs: 5.69 lakh**. **Visakhapatnam Declaration** extended NeSDA to Ladakh and NE. SC: digital access part of **Article 21**.
[94:4:4] ~~ Sources: app notes 4:94 (sub 5)
## SUB 5 | Satellite Communication
[94:5:0] - **INSAT-1B (1983)**: first fully operational INSAT satellite.
[94:5:1] - **GSAT-N2 (GSAT-20)**: broadband satellite launched by **SpaceX** Falcon 9 (Nov 2024).
[94:5:2] ~~ Sources: app notes 4:94 (sub 6)
## SUB 6 | AP Digital Connectivity
[94:6:0] - **APSFL:** **IP-MPLS ring** (over GPON); **triple play** (internet, IPTV, voice); 6,300+ cable operators.
[94:6:1] - **AP FiberNet Phase 3:** **4,000+ tribal hamlets** in Eastern Ghats.
[94:6:2] - **Amended BharatNet MoC (Feb 2026):** **3,942 villages**; every household in **18 months**; CM asked operators to finish new towers in 100 days.
[94:6:3] - **RTGS:** formed **2017**; data-driven e-governance under the CMO.
[94:6:4] ~~ Sources: app notes 4:94 (sub 8)

=== NOTES 4:95 | Transport in Andhra Pradesh – Highways, Railways, Ports, Airports and Waterways
## SUB 0 | Ports of AP – Framework, Districts and Models
[95:0:0] - **Coastline 1,053.07 km** = **third-longest** (after Gujarat, Tamil Nadu; 2023-24 remeasurement); older **974 km = second** (APPSC 2022 key).
[95:0:1] - **1 major port** (Visakhapatnam, GoI) + **16-17 notified non-major ports** under the **AP Maritime Board (APMB)**, 3 captive.
[95:0:2] - Major ports = **Union List Entry 27**; non-major (Other Than Major Ports) = **Concurrent List Entry 31**.
[95:0:3] TABLE head: Port | District | Model / operator | Feature
  [95:0:3.0] Visakhapatnam | Visakhapatnam | Major; Visakhapatnam Port Authority (GoI) | 141.60 MTPA; record 91.17 MMT (2025-26, +10.34%)
  [95:0:3.1] Gangavaram | Visakhapatnam | PPP-BOOT (2003); ops 17 Apr 2009 | Greenfield deep-water port; deepest draft; 64 MMT capacity; "largest private-sector port" (UPSC 2009)
  [95:0:3.2] Krishnapatnam | SPSR Nellore | PPP-BOST (2004); ops 20 Mar 2009 | Largest private port in AP; highest cargo 41.09 MMT (to Nov 2025)
  [95:0:3.3] Kakinada Deep Water | Kakinada | PPP-OMST; Kakinada Seaports Ltd (from 1999) | Highest Govt revenue share, 22% of gross income; farm exports, chemicals, fertilisers
  [95:0:3.4] Kakinada Anchorage | Kakinada | State-run by APMB (not PPP DBFOT) | Lighterage; rice exports; ~4 MMT
  [95:0:3.5] Rawa (Ravva) | Kakinada region (S. Yanam) | Captive non-major; Cairn SBM | Offshore oil and gas
  [95:0:3.6] Kakinada Gateway | Kakinada | DBFOT; Kakinada SEZ-linked | 16 MTPA
[95:0:4] - **Kakinada** = AP's **port city**: Anchorage Port, Deep Water Port (State-built 1997), Fishing Harbour, Ship-Breaking Unit.
[95:0:5] - **Krishnapatnam** (Nellore) benefits AP most; its rail outlet is the Obulavaripalle-Venkatachalam line.
[95:0:6] - Non-major cargo 2025-26 (to Nov 2025): **80.82 MMT**; Krishnapatnam 41.09, Gangavaram 24.39, Kakinada DWP 12.93; ₹252.21 crore to the State.
[95:0:7] - **PYQ 2024 key:** Krishnapatnam-**Nellore**, Gangavaram-**Visakhapatnam**, Ramayapatnam-**Prakasam**, **Bhavanapadu-Srikakulam**.
[95:0:8] - AP has 3 national industrial corridors + NH-16: an **east-coast gateway to South-East Asia**. **Blue economy target: US$ 20 bn by 2035.**
[95:0:9] ~~ Sources: AP Socio-Economic Survey 2024-25 p158-159; NextGen Rocket Sheets AP Geography p51-52; app notes 4:95 (sub 5, 6)
## SUB 1 | Visakhapatnam Port
[95:1:0] - Opened to ocean traffic in **October 1933** with the arrival of the passenger ship **SS Jaladurga**.
[95:1:1] - **Deepest land-locked and protected port** (NextGen; APPSC 2025 key); natural + artificial harbour parts; called the **"Brightest Jewel"**.
[95:1:2] - Protected by **Dolphin's Nose** (Yarada hills, ~335 m) from waves and cyclones.
[95:1:3] - **Outer harbour** for **iron ore exports**; crude oil berth; all-weather, deep-water port.
[95:1:4] - Ranked **third** in port traffic (FY 2022 key). Helped transport of agricultural products (APPSC 2022 key; the port itself is a 1933, 20th-century work).
[95:1:5] - **Top seafood-export port** (2025-26): **5.12 lakh tonnes**, over **26%** of India's 19.32 lakh tonnes.
[95:1:6] - First direct **South-East India – North Europe (IEX)** service, also linking the Mediterranean.
[95:1:7] - Deepest port = Visakhapatnam; deepest draft = Gangavaram.
[95:1:8] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Geography 11 p6-7; NextGen Rocket Sheets AP Geography p50; app notes 4:95 (sub 7); SS Jaladurga, IEX: general knowledge, not checked
## SUB 2 | Greenfield and Proposed Ports
[95:2:0] TABLE head: Greenfield port | District | Capacity / progress (Oct 2025)
  [95:2:0.0] Ramayapatnam | Prakasam (exam key) → SPSR Nellore (Survey 2025-26) | 34.04 MTPA; 74.34% (highest)
  [95:2:0.1] Mulapeta (formerly Bhavanapadu) | Srikakulam – between Visakhapatnam and Paradip | 23.5 MTPA; 61.91%; Vishwa Samudra
  [95:2:0.2] Machilipatnam | Krishna | 35 MTPA; 48.35%; MEIL
  [95:2:0.3] Kakinada Gateway | Kakinada | 16 MTPA
[95:2:1] - **Four greenfield ports** (Visakhapatnam is not one): **₹17,067 crore**; add **108 MMTPA**; cut logistics costs **30-50%**; Phase-I operations by **Dec 2026**.
[95:2:2] - Port capacity target: **145 MTPA by 2030**, **300 MTPA by 2047**.
[95:2:3] - **Dugarajapatnam** (Duggirajupatnam): **major port** promised in the **13th Schedule** of the AP Reorganisation Act (GoI to develop it); was in **SPSR Nellore**, now **Tirupati** district; MDL merchant shipyard (₹15,000 crore, 2026).
[95:2:4] - **Vodarevu and Nizampatnam** stalled by a **CBI probe**. Future ports: Bheemunipatnam, Kalingapatnam, Narsapur.
[95:2:5] ~~ Sources: NextGen Rocket Sheets AP Geography p51-52; AP Reorganisation Act 2014, 13th Schedule; CARE APPSC CA Sep 2026 p27-28; app notes 4:95 (sub 5, 8)
## SUB 3 | Airports of AP
[95:3:0] TABLE head: Airport | Category | Fact
  [95:3:0.0] Visakhapatnam | International | Busiest: 30.95 lakh passengers (FY 2024-25); civil enclave at INS Dega; Colombo flight July 2017
  [95:3:0.1] Vijayawada (Gannavaram) | International | DIGI YATRA; new terminal; Varanasi flight (SpiceJet, Feb 2017)
  [95:3:0.2] Tirupati (Renigunta) | International (since Oct 2015) | Runway 3,810 m
  [95:3:0.3] Rajahmundry | Domestic | Runway 3,165 m
  [95:3:0.4] Kadapa | Domestic | Opened 7 Jun 2015; UDAN
  [95:3:0.5] Orvakal (Kurnool) | Domestic | Uyyalawada Narasimha Reddy Airport; operational 25 Mar 2021 (flights from 28 Mar 2021); first South Indian airport built fully by a State government
[95:3:1] - **Six** operational airports before Aug 2026; **three international** (Visakhapatnam, Vijayawada, Tirupati). **Puttaparthi** = private airstrip of the Sri Sathya Sai Trust, **not among the six**.
[95:3:2] - AP airports lost ₹730 crore in five years (2017-18 to 2021-22); Vijayawada alone ₹272 crore.
[95:3:3] # Bhogapuram
[95:3:4] - **Alluri Sitarama Raju International Airport**, Bhogapuram, **Vizianagaram**; ~50 km from Visakhapatnam.
[95:3:5] - Developer **GMR (GVIAL)**, **PPP-DBFOT** (not an NIIF corpus); ~₹5,640 crore; **6 Mppa**, expandable to **40 Mppa**.
[95:3:6] - **Inaugurated by PM 1 Aug 2026**; commercial operations **17 Aug 2026** (code VTZ); replaced the INS Dega civil enclave.
[95:3:7] - **AP's first greenfield airport since 2014.** Nodal SPV: **APADCL** (formerly BIACL), **100% State-owned**.
[95:3:8] - **Proposed:** **Dagadarthi** (Nellore; no-frills PPP, cargo hub), **Kuppam, Srikakulam**, Ongole, Nagarjuna Sagar, Tuni-Annavaram, Tadepalligudem, **Amaravati (4,500 acres)**. Gannavaram is the existing Vijayawada airport.
[95:3:9] - **Seaplane** aerodromes planned at ten sites incl. **Prakasam Barrage**.
[95:3:10] - **Cochin (1999)** = first airport built by a **public limited company** under PPP.
[95:3:11] - **History:** Tata's Karachi-Madras air service linked to **Hyderabad (Hakimpet)** in **1935**.
[95:3:12] ~~ Sources: AP Socio-Economic Survey 2024-25 p11, p154-155; app notes 4:95 (sub 9); Hakimpet 1935, SpiceJet and Colombo flights: general knowledge, not checked
## SUB 4 | Roads, National Highways and APSRTC
[95:4:0] TABLE head: Category | Length
  [95:4:0.0] National Highways | 8,744 km (55 NHs; Survey 2024-25; older key: 38 NHs)
  [95:4:0.1] State Highways | 12,653 km
  [95:4:0.2] Major District Roads | 26,527 km
  [95:4:0.3] Other District Roads | 6,253 km
  [95:4:0.4] R&B roads excl. NHs (2024-25) | 45,379 km (undivided AP, March 2006: 63,639 km)
  [95:4:0.5] Total roads (Dec 2025) | 54,177 km
  [95:4:0.6] Rural roads (PRED, Apr 2025) | 79,452 km
[95:4:1] - Roads carry **more than 80%** of AP's traffic. NH density **17.64 km per lakh population** (India 12.09); 53.65 km/1,000 sq km (India 44.46).
[95:4:2] - NH control: NHAI 4,406 km; MoRTH-PIU 600 km; State PWD 3,738 km. Longest State Highway: **SH-31** (exam key).
[95:4:3] - **PMGSY** covered **99.09%** of habitations. **APRDC** set up by the **AP State Road Development Corporation Act, 1998**.
[95:4:4] - **APSRTC:** from the Nizam's road transport (1932); named APSRTC **11 Jan 1958**. **Stree Shakti** = free bus travel for **women and transgender persons** from **15 Aug 2025**; **750 e-buses** under **PM e-Bus Sewa** (+300 for Tirupati-Tirumala).
[95:4:5] - **Setu Bandhan:** Union Government (MoRTH) scheme under which Rail Over Bridges (ROBs) are being built in AP.
[95:4:6] TABLE head: NH | Route | Note
  [95:4:6.0] NH-16 | Chennai-Kolkata (old NH-5) | Longest NH in AP, ~1,000 km in State; part of Golden Quadrilateral, whose longest stretch (1,014 km) is in AP
  [95:4:6.1] NH-44 | Srinagar-Kanyakumari | Crosses Rayalaseema via Kurnool, Anantapur; India's longest NH
  [95:4:6.2] NH-65 | Pune-Machilipatnam (old NH-9) | Old NH-9 = Pune-Vijayawada via Sholapur, Hyderabad
  [95:4:6.3] NH-544G | Bengaluru-Kadapa-Vijayawada Economic Corridor | Due by 2027; NHAI set 4 Guinness records (Jan 2026)

## SUB 5 | Railways – South Coast Railway, Lines and Tunnels
[95:5:0] - **First railway in Andhra: Puttur-Renigunta, 1862** (Madras Railway; a 1864 "Renigunta-Reddipalli" key also uses Renigunta). AP network **100% electrified**; works worth ₹92,649 crore under way (3 Feb 2026).
[95:5:1] - **South Coast Railway:** mandated by the **AP Reorganisation Act, 2014**; announced 2019; carved from **SCR and ECoR**; **India's 18th zone**, **HQ Visakhapatnam**, operational **1 Jun 2026**.
[95:5:2] TABLE head: SCoR division | Came from
  [95:5:2.0] Visakhapatnam | Truncated Waltair division of ECoR
  [95:5:2.1] Vijayawada | SCR
  [95:5:2.2] Guntur | SCR
  [95:5:2.3] Guntakal | SCR
[95:5:3] - **SCR keeps:** **Hyderabad, Secunderabad (HQ), Nanded**. Rest of Waltair → ECoR's new **Rayagada** division.
[95:5:4] TABLE head: New line | Key facts
  [95:5:4.0] Errupalem-Nambur (Amaravati line) | 57 km, ₹2,245 crore; NTR and Guntur districts (AP) + Khammam (Telangana); 3-km Krishna bridge; 4-year timeline
  [95:5:4.1] Nadikudi-Srikalahasti | 308 km; four backward districts Guntur, Prakasam, Nellore, Chittoor; cement and granite traffic
  [95:5:4.2] Kotipalli-Narasapur | Konaseema coast; in progress (Feb 2026)
  [95:5:4.3] 3rd/4th lines Rajahmundry-Visakhapatnam | ₹9,889 crore
[95:5:5] - APPSC G2 2025: the "**6-year timeline**" for the Amaravati line was the **WRONG** statement.
[95:5:6] - **Cherlopalli-Rapuru tunnel:** **6.6 km**, SPSR Nellore; **India's longest electrified rail tunnel when opened (2019)**, built by **South Central Railway**; on the **Obulavaripalle-Venkatachalam** line linking **Rayalaseema to Krishnapatnam port**.
[95:5:7] - **Port rail links:** Ramayapatnam next to **NH-16 and Thettu** station; Kakinada Gateway has its own line; last-mile rail to **Kopparthy, Orvakal, Krishnapatnam** nodes incomplete.
[95:5:8] # Stations and trains
[95:5:9] TABLE head: Station / train | Feature
  [95:5:9.0] Vijayawada station | IGBC Platinum green certification (not LEED, 2023); second in SCR after Secunderabad; BIPV rooftop solar
  [95:5:9.1] Net-zero stations | Vijayawada division: ten "Shunya Plus" stations incl. Dwarapudi, Kadiyam (Aug 2026)

[95:5:10] - **Amrit Bharat:** **21 of SCR's 73** stations are in AP.

## SUB 6 | Inland Waterways and Canals
[95:6:0] - **NW-4** = Kakinada-Puducherry canal system with the **Godavari and Krishna** (1,095 km); AP stretch Peddaganjam-Tada 895 km.
[95:6:1] - **Canals:** **Kakinada, Eluru, Commamur, North and South Buckingham** (**not Krishna Canal**).
[95:6:2] TABLE head: River | NW-4 stretch
  [95:6:2.0] Godavari | Bhadrachalam-Dowleswaram
  [95:6:2.1] Krishna | Wazirabad-Prakasam Barrage
[95:6:3] - **AP's first riverine project: Muktyala-Madipadu** on the Krishna (APPSC G1 2024). IWAI finished Krishna surveys (Mar 2026).
[95:6:4] - **Buckingham Canal:** ~796 km; **longest canal of commercial importance** on the east coast; flows through **Pulicat Lake**.
[95:6:5] - Industrial Policy 2024-29 plans NW-4 for modal shift.
[95:6:6] ~~ Sources: NextGen AP Geography 03 p17-18; app notes 4:95 (sub 10)
## SUB 7 | Logistics, Sagarmala and Metro
[95:7:0] - **Nexus Integrated Logistics Park:** Chinna Eetivakam (Satyavedu, Tirupati); 63.01 acres, ₹255 crore, ~1,000 jobs; built through **APMB = AP Maritime Board** (not Medical Board).
[95:7:1] - **APLINC** (AP Logistics Infrastructure Corporation) = holding company. **AP Logistics Fund** = AIF, State sponsor up to 20%.
[95:7:2] - Bhogapuram aerotropolis: 25,000 t cargo capacity.
[95:7:3] - **Sagarmala pillars:** port modernisation, connectivity, port-led industrialisation, coastal community development, coastal shipping/IWT (**DFCs not a pillar**).
[95:7:4] - AP asks: Sagarmala roads to Gangavaram + AM/NS captive port; Sagarmala 2.0 funds for fishing harbours (Juvvaladinne 97.98%).
[95:7:5] - **Visakhapatnam Metro:** **4 corridors, 76.70 km**; metro-cum-flyover on NH-16. State says Vizag + Vijayawada metros approved (27 Jan 2026); Centre still checking.
[95:7:6] - **Vizhinjam** (Kerala, May 2025) = India's first deep-water transshipment and first semi-automated container port.
[95:7:7] - Challenges: logistics cost, no dedicated freight rail to clusters, water-short inland nodes, CBI-stalled ports, land delays.
[95:7:8] ~~ Sources: app notes 4:95 (sub 11)

=== NOTES 4:98 | Services Sector Geography - Tourism (india and Ap), It, Banking
## SUB 0 | UNESCO World Heritage Sites – India and the World
[98:0:0] # India's count
[98:0:1] - **India: 45 WHS** (July 2026) – **37 cultural, 7 natural, 1 mixed**; **6th in the world**. **Andhra Pradesh has none**.
[98:0:2] - **44 in July 2025** (Maratha Military Landscapes, 47th session, Paris) → **45th = Sarnath** (UP's 4th), 48th session, **Busan**, July 2026.
[98:0:3] - Maratha Military Landscapes: **12 forts – 11 in Maharashtra + Gingee (Tamil Nadu)**.
[98:0:4] - Earliest (1983): **Ajanta, Ellora (Maharashtra)**, Agra Fort, Taj Mahal. India ratified the Convention in 1977.
[98:0:5] - Only **mixed** site: **Khangchendzonga NP, Sikkim** (2016). Only Harappan WHS: **Dholavira** (2021).
[98:0:6] # Inscriptions asked
[98:0:7] TABLE head: Year | Inscription | State
  [98:0:7.0] 1983 | Ajanta, Ellora | Maharashtra
  [98:0:7.1] 1984 | Konark Sun Temple (Eastern Ganga Narasimha Deva I, 1238-64; builder Bisu Moharana); Mamallapuram | Odisha; Tamil Nadu
  [98:0:7.2] 1986 / 1989 / 2003 | Khajuraho / Sanchi / Bhimbetka | Madhya Pradesh
  [98:0:7.3] 2002 | Mahabodhi Temple, Bodh Gaya (cultural) | Bihar
  [98:0:7.4] 2004 | Champaner-Pavagadh (Panchmahal); Chhatrapati Shivaji Terminus | Gujarat; Maharashtra
  [98:0:7.5] 2010 | Jantar Mantar, Jaipur (28th site) | Rajasthan
  [98:0:7.6] 2012 | Western Ghats (natural) – includes Kaas Plateau, Satara | Maharashtra–Kerala
  [98:0:7.7] 2014 | Rani-ki-Vav | Gujarat
  [98:0:7.8] 2016 | Khangchendzonga (35th, mixed); Nalanda | Sikkim; Bihar
  [98:0:7.9] 2017 | Historic City of Ahmedabad (36th) | Gujarat
  [98:0:7.10] 2018 | Victorian Gothic and Art Deco Ensembles of Mumbai (37th) | Maharashtra
  [98:0:7.11] 2019 | Jaipur City – "Pink City", "Paris of India" | Rajasthan
  [98:0:7.12] 2021 | Ramappa; Dholavira | Telangana; Gujarat
  [98:0:7.13] 2023 | Santiniketan (Birbhum, Tagore); Sacred Ensembles of the Hoysalas – only these two | West Bengal; Karnataka
  [98:0:7.14] 2024 | Moidams of the Ahom dynasty | Assam
  [98:0:7.15] 2025 | Maratha Military Landscapes (44th) | Maharashtra, TN
  [98:0:7.16] 2026 | Sarnath (45th) | Uttar Pradesh
[98:0:8] - Natural sites: Kaziranga, Manas, Sundarbans, Western Ghats, Nanda Devi and Valley of Flowers.
[98:0:9] - **NOT WHS:** **Mandu fort** (MP), Buland Darwaza, Charminar.
[98:0:10] - **Kaas Plateau ("Kaas Pathar")**: volcanic plateau in **Satara, Maharashtra** (not Karnataka), WHS in **2012** with the Western Ghats (not 2019).
[98:0:11] # World Heritage City
[98:0:12] - **Ahmedabad = India's first World Heritage City (2017)**; Jaipur second (2019). Varanasi, Hyderabad, Delhi not inscribed.
[98:0:13] - **Tentative List:** Delhi, Ahmedabad and Jaipur city nominations were on it (Ahmedabad and Jaipur since inscribed); **Tirupati never was**.
[98:0:14] - AP on the Tentative List: **Sri Veerabhadra Temple and Monolithic Nandi, Lepakshi** (Sri Sathya Sai district), added **2022**.
[98:0:15] # World sites asked
[98:0:16] - **Plitvice Lakes NP, Croatia** – WHS 1979; sixteen lakes linked by waterfalls.
[98:0:17] - **Prosecco Hills** of Conegliano and Valdobbiadene, **Italy** – inscribed 2019.
[98:0:18] - ICOMOS (1965, Paris) evaluates cultural nominations; India on the World Heritage Committee 2021-25.
[98:0:19] > Exam angle: count (44 in 2025 → 45 in 2026), "which year" (2023 = Santiniketan + Hoysalas only), first heritage city (Ahmedabad), "which is NOT a WHS" (Mandu).

[98:1:14] - **Indira Gandhi Rashtriya Manav Sangrahalaya** (National Museum of Mankind) – **Shamla Hills, Bhopal**.
[98:1:15] - **Statue of Unity**: **182 m**, Kevadia (Ekta Nagar), Sardar Patel; sculptor **Ram V. Sutar**.

[98:2:3] # Caves
[98:2:4] - **Borra Caves**: Ananthagiri hills, Araku (**ASR**, near **Visakhapatnam**); **karstic limestone**, **stalactites and stalagmites**, "million-year-old"; found **1807 by William King George**; SD 2.0 project Rs 29.87 crore.
[98:2:5] - **Belum Caves**: **Kolimigundla mandal** (was Kurnool – **now Nandyal**); **2nd longest in India**; first recorded by **Robert Bruce Foote, 1884**; GSI geo-heritage site (2025).
[98:2:6] - Undavalli caves: Guntur; rock-cut, reclining Vishnu.
[98:2:7] # Temples and pilgrim centres

  [98:2:8.0] Tirumala | Venkateswara | Seshachalam, Tirupati

  [98:2:8.2] Yaganti | Uma Maheswara; growing Nandi | Erramala, Nandyal
  [98:2:8.3] Kanaka Durga | Durga | Indrakeeladri, Vijayawada
  [98:2:8.4] Srikalahasti | Vayu linga | Swarnamukhi, Tirupati
  [98:2:8.5] Simhachalam | Varaha Lakshmi Narasimha; 96 black-stone pillars; Chandanotsavam | Visakhapatnam
  [98:2:8.6] Annavaram | Satyanarayana | Ratnagiri, Pampa; Kakinada
  [98:2:8.7] Ahobilam | Nine Narasimha shrines | Nallamala; was Kurnool – now Nandyal
[98:2:9] - **Tirupati** (13.65°N 79.42°E; was Chittoor – now Tirupati district): "**Spiritual Capital of AP**"; Ministry of Tourism "**Best Heritage City" 2012-13** (award 18 Feb 2014).

## SUB 3 | Tourism Schemes, Campaigns, Luxury Trains and Projects (India)
[98:3:0] # Campaigns, taglines, days
[98:3:1] - **Incredible India** – Ministry of Tourism's international campaign, **2002**; Atithi Devo Bhavah 2008; e-Tourist Visa 2014; Ministry of Tourism separate since 2000.
[98:3:2] - State taglines: **Karnataka – "One State, Many Worlds"**; Kerala – God's Own Country; Rajasthan – Padharo Mhare Desh; MP – Heart of Incredible India; Odisha – Soul of Incredible India; AP – Essence of Incredible India.
[98:3:3] - **World Tourism Day** (27 Sept) **2019 host: India** (New Delhi).
[98:3:4] - **World's most visited country: France** (UNWTO 2006 and still in 2024), then Spain, USA.
[98:3:5] # Schemes
[98:3:6] TABLE head: Scheme | Key fact
  [98:3:6.0] Swadesh Darshan 1.0 (Jan 2015; 2014-15) | Theme-based circuits; 76 projects, Rs 5,290.33 cr
  [98:3:6.1] Swadesh Darshan 2.0 | Sustainable, destination-centric; 53 projects (July 2026); Central Sector
  [98:3:6.2] CBDD (sub-scheme of SD 2.0) | Challenge Based Destination Development: spiritual, culture and heritage, Vibrant Village, ecotourism, Amrit Dharohar
  [98:3:6.3] PRASHAD | Pilgrimage Rejuvenation and Spiritual, Heritage Augmentation Drive – only pilgrimage sites; 54 projects
  [98:3:6.4] Dekho Apna Desh | Domestic tourism; People's Choice 2024 poll
  [98:3:6.5] NIDHI | National Integrated Database of Hospitality Industry – database of hospitality units, Ministry of Tourism
  [98:3:6.6] SASCI | Special Assistance to States for Capital Investment – iconic centres into global destinations
  [98:3:6.7] Adarsh Smarak (2014-15) | Model monuments; AP: Amaravati, Nagarjunakonda, Bavikonda
  [98:3:6.8] Adopt a Heritage (2017) | "Apni Dharohar, Apni Pehchaan"; companies adopt sites
  [98:3:6.9] HRIDAY | Heritage city development
[98:3:7] - SD 1.0 themes: Buddhist, Coastal, Desert, Eco, Heritage, Himalayan, Krishna, North-East, Ramayana, Rural, Spiritual, Sufi, Tirthankar, Tribal, Wildlife.
[98:3:8] - AP under PRASHAD: **Tirupati, Srisailam, Simhachalam, Annavaram**.
[98:3:9] - Swadesh Darshan = thematic circuits; PRASHAD = pilgrimage sites (common swap).

[98:3:17] - **Gujarat's submarine tourism** (off **Dwarka**): with **Mazagon Dock Shipbuilders Ltd** (G2 2024). Maharashtra plans an underwater museum (INS Guldar) off Sindhudurg (2026).
[98:3:18] - Visakhapatnam: **INS Kursura** submarine museum.
[98:3:19] - **Medical tourism**: share in FTAs 2.2% (2009) to ~6.5% (2024); top source **Bangladesh**.
[98:3:20] - **MICE** (meetings, incentives, conferences, exhibitions): high-yield, low-volume; AP draft MICE hubs Visakhapatnam, Vijayawada.

## SUB 5 | AP Tourism Policy 2024-29, Bodies and Statistics
[98:5:0] # Bodies
[98:5:1] - **APTDC**: began **Feb 1976 as a subsidiary of APSRTC** (Travel and Tourism Development Corporation); re-registered as AP Travel and Tourism Development Corporation Ltd (1980); **independent in 1981** (APPSC G2 2011 key, not verified); bifurcated 2 June 2014.
[98:5:2] - **APTA** = nodal (branding, promotion, investment, destination management); **APTDC** = operational State PSU (Haritha hotels, packages, boating, PPP; 39 hotels, 843 rooms).
[98:5:3] # Policy 2024-29
[98:5:4] - **Industry status**; up to **25% fixed capital subsidy**, industrial power tariff, **100% net SGST reimbursement**.
[98:5:5] TABLE head: Anchor hub (7) | Theme
  [98:5:5.0] Visakhapatnam | Beach, cruise
  [98:5:5.1] Tirupati | Spiritual
  [98:5:5.2] Araku | Hill, eco; coffee, tribal
  [98:5:5.3] Rajahmundry | River
  [98:5:5.4] Amaravati | Heritage, Buddhist
  [98:5:5.5] Srisailam | Pilgrimage, eco
  [98:5:5.6] Gandikota | Heritage, adventure
[98:5:6] - **Kakinada is NOT** an anchor hub.
[98:5:7] - **25+ circuits**: 2 Buddhist, 10 temple, **5 beach** (1,053 km coast), 4 river cruise, 3 eco, 2 sea cruise, seaplane.
[98:5:8] - Proposals Rs 19,693 crore; central sanction Rs 206.77 crore (SD 2.0 + PRASHAD); Best Tourism Promotion State, GTM 2026.
[98:5:9] - Direct jobs: hotels, transport, guides; indirect: supply chains.
[98:5:10] - AP sought **CRZ-3 to CRZ-2** reclassification of Vizag Beach Road.
[98:5:11] - New districts (31 Dec 2025): **Polavaram** (HQ Rampachodavaram), **Markapuram** – AP 28 districts.
[98:5:12] # AP statistics
[98:5:13] - Arrivals: **28.58 crore (2023-24) to 36.42 crore (2025-26)**; foreign 1.70 lakh to 2.72 lakh (~49%).
[98:5:14] - AP = **3rd most visited State for domestic tourism** (2024-25; also 3rd in 2022).
[98:5:15] > Exam angle: APTDC history (1976 / 1981), anchor hubs, incentives.

[98:8:4] - **PMJDY**: launched **28 August 2014**; over **54 crore** accounts.

[98:8:6] - AP banking (31 Dec 2024): most branches **Visakhapatnam (624)**; fewest **Alluri Sitharama Raju (52)**. AP services GVA growth 8.53% (2024-25).

[98:8:8] - **STPI units** and **Global Capability Centres** spread IT to Tier-2 and Tier-3 cities.
[98:8:9] - AP IT and GCC Policy 4.0 (2024-29): co-working and neighbourhood workspaces in Tirupati, Visakhapatnam, Vijayawada.
[98:8:10] - **Google AI data centre**: **Visakhapatnam and Anakapalli**, **US$ 15 bn** (~Rs 87,520 crore) – Google's largest AI hub outside the US. TCS and Cognizant began operations in Vizag (2025-26).
[98:8:11] - APEITA = nodal agency for AP's IT and Electronics policies.
[98:8:12] ~~ Sources: app notes 4:98 (sub 0); AP Socio-Economic Survey 2024-25 p.169 (APEITA)
## SUB 9 | Heritage Acts and ASI
[98:9:0] - **ASI** conserves **3,688** centrally protected monuments (3,697 in Feb 2024 Lok Sabha reply).
[98:9:1] - **AMASR Act 1958**; **Antiquities and Art Treasures Act 1972**.
[98:9:2] - **20 new sites** identified for the **national importance** tag (Lok Sabha, 2 Aug 2022, Culture Minister **G. Kishan Reddy**) – e.g. Rakhigarhi and Anangtal mounds, **Chintakunta rock paintings (AP)**.

=== NOTES 4:99 | Energy Resources - Coal, Petroleum, Gas, Hydel, Nuclear, Renewable; India and Ap
## SUB 0 | Petroleum and Natural Gas – Oilfields, Refineries, Reserves
[99:0:0] # Firsts and oilfields
[99:0:1] - **First petroleum detection:** Margherita (Assam). **First oil well:** **Makum**, upper Assam, **26 March 1867** (118 ft) – not Digboi.
[99:0:2] - **Digboi** (Tinsukia, Assam) = first commercially successful oil discovery, production from **1889**; **oldest oil field** and oldest oil-producing area (Assam = oldest oil State).
[99:0:3] - **Naharkatiya** lies about **32 km south-west of Digboi**; Naharkatia-Noonmati-Barauni = India's **first** (Asia's first cross-country) oil pipeline.
[99:0:4] - World: first commercial oil output recorded in **Romania (1857)**; USA (Drake well) 1859.
[99:0:5] TABLE head: Field / basin | State
  [99:0:5.0] Digboi, Makum, Naharkatiya, Moran-Hugrijan | Assam (Brahmaputra valley)
  [99:0:5.1] Ankleshwar (Bharuch), Kalol, Mehsana, Navagam, Kosamba, Lunej (Cambay basin) | Gujarat
  [99:0:5.2] Mumbai High (offshore, about 160 km off Mumbai; found by Indian-Russian survey from 1964-65, oil struck 1974) | Arabian Sea – largest oil and largest natural-gas producer
  [99:0:5.3] Barmer (Mangala, Bhagyam, Aishwariya) | Rajasthan – largest onshore producer
  [99:0:5.4] KG basin (KG-D6 = gas; Rawa field) | Andhra Pradesh – crude from Rawa exported via a single buoy mooring
[99:0:6] - Kalol is in Gujarat, **not Assam**. Exploration: **ONGC** and **Oil India Ltd** (HPCL and IOC refine and market).
[99:0:7] - Shale gas: Cambay basin has the largest estimate; also Assam-Arakan, Gondwana (Damodar), KG, Cauvery.
[99:0:8] - Oil trap (anticline under cap rock), bottom to top: **water → oil → gas/air** (by density).
[99:0:9] - India imports about **88%** of its crude. Hormuz closure (March 2026): India fell back on coal and gas and bought LNG and LPG from other suppliers.
[99:0:10] # Refineries
[99:0:11] - India = **4th largest refiner**; capacity 258.1 MMTPA (June 2026). Field-based (**Digboi**) vs market-based (Barauni, Mathura).
[99:0:12] TABLE head: Refinery | State | Operator / year
  [99:0:12.0] Digboi | Assam | IOCL; 1901 – oldest
  [99:0:12.1] Noonmati (Guwahati) | Assam | IOCL; 1962 – first public-sector
  [99:0:12.2] Numaligarh | Golaghat, Assam | NRL
  [99:0:12.3] Bongaigaon | Assam | IOCL
  [99:0:12.4] Jamnagar | Gujarat | Reliance – biggest in India and the world
  [99:0:12.5] Koyali (Vadodara) | Gujarat | IOCL; 1965
  [99:0:12.6] Vadinar | Gujarat | Nayara Energy
  [99:0:12.7] Barauni | Bihar | IOCL
  [99:0:12.8] Mathura | UP | IOCL; 1982
  [99:0:12.9] Haldia | WB | IOCL; 1975
  [99:0:12.10] Bina | MP | BPCL
  [99:0:12.11] Kochi / Mangalore (MRPL) / Mumbai | Kerala / Karnataka / Maharashtra | BPCL / ONGC-MRPL / BPCL, HPCL
  [99:0:12.12] Manali | Chennai, TN | CPCL
  [99:0:12.13] Panipat / Paradip | Haryana / Odisha | IOCL
  [99:0:12.14] Barmer (Pachpadra) | Rajasthan | HPCL-Rajasthan Govt JV
  [99:0:12.15] Visakhapatnam | AP | HPCL
  [99:0:12.16] Tatipaka | AP (Konaseema) | ONGC mini refinery
[99:0:13] - **Hatia (Jharkhand) has no refinery.** South to north on the west coast: **Kochi – Mangalore – Mumbai – Koyali**.
[99:0:14] - Oil companies' taglines: IOC "The Energy of India"; BPCL "Energising Lives". In 2016-17 **IOC** overtook ONGC as the most profitable PSU.
[99:0:15] # Strategic reserves and gas grid
[99:0:16] - **ISPRL** SPRs: **Visakhapatnam, Mangaluru, Padur** – 5.33 MMT (about 9.5 days); Phase-II **Chandikhol** + Padur. **Ankleshwar was not selected.**
[99:0:17] - **PM Urja Ganga** gas pipeline: **Jagdishpur–Haldia–Bokaro–Dhamra**; national gas grid target over 32,000 km.
[99:0:18] - Petronet LNG terminals: Dahej, Kochi; Dredging Corporation of India HQ = **Visakhapatnam**.
[99:0:19] # World
[99:0:20] TABLE head: Item | Leader
  [99:0:20.0] Proved oil reserves | Venezuela (303.8 bn barrels), Saudi Arabia, Canada, Iran, Iraq, Kuwait (2023) – Saudi Arabia was 1st till about 2010
  [99:0:20.1] Oil producer | USA, then Saudi Arabia, Russia
  [99:0:20.2] Gas reserves | Russia, Iran, Qatar (not Qatar first)
  [99:0:20.3] Gas producer | USA, Russia
[99:0:21] - Middle East holds over half (older books: about 60%) of world oil reserves; USA is a big producer and importer; Alaska (bought from Russia, 1867) = USA's oil State.
[99:0:22] > Exam angle: Makum (first well), Digboi (oldest field 1889, oldest refinery 1901), Naharkatiya, Kalol ≠ Assam, Tatipaka = AP (ONGC), Hatia no refinery, SPR sites.
[99:0:23] ~~ Sources: Rocket Indian Geography p.16 (first oil well Makum, Digboi), p.91-92 (refineries, operators, years); PMF Human & Economic Geography 03 p.32-44 (world reserves, Assam/Gujarat/Rajasthan fields, Mumbai High, Rawa, refinery list, pipelines, ISPRL), p.46 (gas reserves); OnlyIAS NCERT Human Geography 10 (Ch7: Mumbai High 160 km, field- and market-based refineries); app notes 4:99 (sub 3)
## SUB 1 | Fuels – Fossil, Renewable and Commercial
[99:1:0] # Classification
[99:1:1] TABLE head: Class | Sources | Key point
  [99:1:1.0] Renewable (replenished in a short time) | Solar, wind, hydro (energy of water), geothermal (energy of the Earth), tidal, biomass, biogas, biofuel, wood | Basis of the energy transition
  [99:1:1.1] Non-renewable (finite) | Fossil fuels – coal, petroleum (crude oil), natural gas + nuclear fuel (uranium, thorium) | Fossil fuels formed over millions of years from buried organisms
[99:1:2] - **Not fossil fuels:** **solar energy, wood, uranium** (non-renewable **nuclear** fuel), biofuel, biogas. Kerosene, diesel, petrol are petroleum products (fossil-derived).
[99:1:3] - **Ultimate source** of fossil-fuel energy = **the Sun**.
[99:1:4] - Fossil fuels **cannot be prepared in the laboratory** from dead organisms (statement trap: "coal can be made in the lab" is false).
[99:1:5] - Earth's **source function**: supplies water, minerals, fish stocks, **fossil fuels**.
[99:1:6] - **Petroleum products:** petrol (gasoline), diesel, kerosene, **LPG, naphtha, bitumen**, plastics; **coal and anthracite are not** petroleum products. Thermal power plants burn **fossil fuels**; solar plants use radiation, not coal.
[99:1:7] - **Bio-asphalt:** made from **renewable/organic waste**, not fossil-based, eco-friendly road surfacing (Budget 2011-12 duty exemption).
[99:1:8] - **Commercial** sources = **power, coal, oil, gas, hydro-electricity, uranium** (atomic); **non-commercial** = firewood, dung, agricultural waste, **biogas**.
[99:1:9] - Coal, crude oil, natural gas together = **fossil fuels**. Coke and producer gas are manufactured fuels.
[99:1:10] - **First oil crisis: 1973** (OPEC quadrupled prices; 1970s-80s crises). Fossil fuels give about **75% of global GHGs and 90% of CO2**.
[99:1:11] - Energy-transition sources = **solar and wind**; National Solar Mission is under the **NAPCC**.
[99:1:12] > Exam angle: "not a fossil fuel" = solar / wood / uranium; "not renewable" = natural gas / petroleum; commercial vs biogas.
[99:1:13] ~~ Sources: Rocket Chemistry p.33 (fuel source and type: coal fossil, biogas renewable, wood biomass); OnlyIAS NCERT Human Geography 10 (Ch7: conventional and non-conventional sources); PMF Human & Economic Geography 03 p.28-29 (petroleum fossil fuel, products, bitumen); app notes 4:99 (sub 0)
## SUB 2 | Coal – Grades, Gondwana and Tertiary Coal, Coalfields
[99:2:0] # Grades
[99:2:1] TABLE head: Grade | Carbon | Occurrence
  [99:2:1.0] Anthracite | 80-95% (highest) | Only J&K, small
  [99:2:1.1] Bituminous | 40-80% | Most used; Jharkhand, Odisha, WB, Chhattisgarh, MP
  [99:2:1.2] Lignite (brown coal) | 40-55% (NextGen 40-50%; Gujarat lignite about 35%) | High moisture; Neyveli (TN), Palana (Rajasthan), Gujarat, Puducherry, Lakhimpur, Karewa (J&K)
  [99:2:1.3] Peat | under 40% | First stage of wood turning into coal
[99:2:2] # Gondwana vs Tertiary
[99:2:3] TABLE head: Type | Age | Where
  [99:2:3.0] Gondwana (about 98% of reserves) | 250-300 mn yr | Damodar valley (most important), Mahanadi, Son, Godavari, Wardha – over 90% of reserves in these valleys
  [99:2:3.1] Tertiary | 15-60 mn yr | North-East (Assam – Makum; Meghalaya – Mawlong, Garo-Khasi hills; Arunachal – Namchik-Namphuk); lignite of TN, Rajasthan, Gujarat
[99:2:4] - **Neyveli** lignite is **Tertiary** coal; Neyveli = first lignite-fired TPS in South-East Asia (NLC India).
[99:2:5] - **Tamil Nadu** = largest lignite reserves (about 80%) and output (48.65% in 2024-25; Gujarat, Rajasthan next); **Jharkhand has no lignite**. India's lignite 47.36 bn t (2023).
[99:2:6] # Coalfields
[99:2:7] TABLE head: Coalfield | State / fact
  [99:2:7.0] Raniganj | West Bengal; first coal mine 1774; largest total reserves among the old fields (24,633 MT)
  [99:2:7.1] Jharia | Jharkhand (Dhanbad); main coking coal; underground fires; NCERT calls it the largest coalfield (19,430 MT)
  [99:2:7.2] Bokaro, Giridih, Karanpura, Ramgarh | Jharkhand
  [99:2:7.3] Talcher | Angul, Odisha (Mahanadi Coalfields)
  [99:2:7.4] Korba, Bisrampur | Chhattisgarh
  [99:2:7.5] Singrauli | MP-UP border; UP's coal (Sonbhadra)
  [99:2:7.6] Sohagpur, Umaria | MP
  [99:2:7.7] Singareni | Telangana, Godavari valley (SCCL)
  [99:2:7.8] Kamptee, Wardha | Maharashtra
[99:2:8] - Chota Nagpur plateau (Jharkhand, Odisha, WB, Chhattisgarh) gives the bulk of India's coal – about **80%** in older books.
[99:2:9] > Exam angle: Damodar = Gondwana; Neyveli = Tertiary lignite; Mawlong = Meghalaya (not Arunachal); Talcher = Odisha; Raniganj first (1774).
[99:2:10] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Geography APPSC 10 p.4 (grades, belts, lignite in TN, Gujarat, Puducherry, Rajasthan); PMF Human & Economic Geography 03 p.13, 19-22 (lignite 40-55%, Gondwana valleys, Jharia, Raniganj 1774, Talcher, Birampur, Neyveli, Makum, Namchik-Namphuk); Rocket Indian Geography p.24-27 (coalfield-State list); OnlyIAS NCERT Human Geography 10 (Ch7: Jharia largest, Raniganj next); app notes 4:99 (sub 2)
## SUB 3 | Coal – Reserves, Production, Mining and Institutions
[99:3:0] # Reserves and production
[99:3:1] TABLE head: Item | Fact
  [99:3:1.0] Proved (measured) reserves, 1.4.2023 | Jharkhand 55.7 bn t > Odisha 52.0 > Chhattisgarh 37.2, then WB
  [99:3:1.1] Total resources, 1.4.2023 | Odisha 94.5 bn t > Jharkhand 87.8 > Chhattisgarh 80.8 (older books: Jharkhand largest)
  [99:3:1.2] Production | Odisha, Chhattisgarh, Jharkhand, MP lead (2020-21: Chhattisgarh 157.7 MT, Odisha 143, Jharkhand 131.7, MP 125.7); Odisha is the top producer among the usual options
[99:3:2] - Coal meets about **55%** of primary energy and over **70%** of electricity – **the largest source of energy in India**.
[99:3:3] - World: **China** = largest producer and consumer; India **2nd producer and consumer**, **5th in reserves**. Reserves: USA, Russia, Australia (14%), China (13%), India (11%) – China led Australia until about 2018.
[99:3:4] - Hard coal reserves: China far ahead of India; Nepal and New Zealand negligible.
[99:3:5] # Mining
[99:3:6] - **Opencast** = main method. **Rat-hole mining** (Meghalaya): narrow pits dug with simple tools, by **small family groups** ("mining by the family members" – APPSC key); **banned by NGT 2014**.
[99:3:7] - **Coal India Ltd** (1975) = world's largest coal producer; **NLC India** = Neyveli lignite.
[99:3:8] - **Coal Controller's Organisation** (1916): main source of **coal statistics**; monitors **captive coal/lignite blocks**; hears **objections to acquisition of coal-bearing areas** – does **not** police delivery times.
[99:3:9] - Revised **SHAKTI** (May 2025): coal through two windows.
[99:3:10] - Indian coal: high ash (30-45%), low sulphur.
[99:3:11] > Exam angle: "largest reserves" – Jharkhand (proved) vs Odisha (total); Chhattisgarh/Odisha in production; rat-hole = family pits.
[99:3:12] ~~ Sources: Ministry of Coal Annual Report 2023-24 ch.2 (coal.gov.in, state-wise measured and total resources as on 1.4.2023, web search summary); PMF Human & Economic Geography 03 p.15, 18, 22 (world reserves 2021, India 2nd producer, State reserves 2022, production 2019-21); app notes 4:99 (sub 2)
## SUB 4 | Thermal Power and India's Power Mix
[99:4:0] # Power mix
[99:4:1] TABLE head: Item | Fact
  [99:4:1.0] Installed capacity | about 552 GW (Aug 2026); 513,729.69 MW (31 Dec 2025)
  [99:4:1.1] Thermal (31 Dec 2025) | 48.07% – highest share; coal gives over 70% of generation
  [99:4:1.2] Non-fossil share | 51.93% (Dec 2025) – 50% target of 2030 met early
  [99:4:1.3] Solar / Wind / Bio-power | 164.59 GW (largest RE) / 58.14 GW / 11.75 GW (Aug 2026)
  [99:4:1.4] Hydro | about 48 GW (June 2025)
  [99:4:1.5] Nuclear (31 Dec 2025) | 8,780 MW = 1.71% of capacity; about 3% of generation (2006-07: 2.8%)
  [99:4:1.6] Global rank | 3rd in RE, 3rd in solar, 4th in wind
[99:4:2] - Old order (2010s): **Thermal > Hydro > Nuclear/Wind**; now by capacity Thermal > Solar > Wind > Hydro > Nuclear.
[99:4:3] - **Gujarat** = 1st in gross installed capacity and RE (Dec 2025); **Maharashtra** was 1st in the 2000s. Gujarat also 1st in wind (Dec 2025), Tamil Nadu 2nd.
[99:4:4] - All-India thermal **PLF** was about **70-75%** in the mid-2000s; coal plants now run at 60-85%.
[99:4:5] - Targets: 500 GW non-fossil by 2030; **net zero 2070**; gas share 6.7% → 15% by 2030; battery storage about 47 GW by 2032. Peak demand 256 GW (Apr 2026); about 150 GW solar drops after sunset.
[99:4:6] - Geothermal potential about **10,600 MW** (not installed); wind potential **695.5 GW at 120 m**.
[99:4:7] # Thermal power stations

[99:4:9] - Singrauli STPS is in **Sonbhadra (UP)**; Vindhyachal is in **Singrauli district (MP)**.
[99:4:10] - **Fly ash** = by-product of coal-fired **thermal plants** (Indian coal 30-45% ash).
[99:4:11] - UPSC 2023: some coal plants **use seawater** (e.g. Ennore, Mundra), some are in **water-stressed** districts (Korba), many are **private** (Adani, Tata, Reliance) – so **none** of the three "none of them" statements is correct.
[99:4:12] > Exam angle: thermal = largest share; plant-State match (Kudgi, Kahalgaon, Sipat, Dadri, Khargone, Korba); Vindhyachal largest.

## SUB 5 | Nuclear Power
[99:5:0] # Plants
[99:5:1] TABLE head: Plant | District, State | Note
  [99:5:1.0] Tarapur | Palghar, Maharashtra (near Boisar) | 1969, India's first nuclear power station; US-built BWRs
  [99:5:1.1] Rawatbhata (RAPS) | Chittorgarh (near Kota), Rajasthan | 1973, Canadian help; oldest PHWR site; RAPP-7 = 25th unit begun July 2011
  [99:5:1.2] Kaiga | Uttara Kannada, Karnataka | Kaiga-4 = India's 20th reactor
  [99:5:1.3] Kakrapar | Surat, Gujarat | First 700 MW PHWRs
  [99:5:1.4] Narora | UP | PHWR
  [99:5:1.5] Kudankulam | Tirunelveli, Tamil Nadu | Russia, VVER-1000; IGA 1988; 6 units agreed
  [99:5:1.6] Kalpakkam (MAPS) | Chengalpattu, TN | Atomic power station; first fully indigenous PHWR; PFBR critical 6 April 2026
  [99:5:1.7] Kovvada | Srikakulam, AP | USA (AP1000), proposed
  [99:5:1.8] Jaitapur | Ratnagiri, Maharashtra | France, 6 EPRs
  [99:5:1.9] Mithi Virdi | Bhavnagar, Gujarat | USA
  [99:5:1.10] Haripur | West Bengal (not Haryana) | Proposed
[99:5:2] - First reactor: **Apsara** (August 1956, BARC Trombay) – Asia's first research reactor.
[99:5:3] - World's first nuclear electricity: **EBR-I, USA (1951)**; first grid-connected station: Obninsk, USSR (1954); first commercial-scale: Calder Hall, UK (1956).
[99:5:4] - **Heavy-water plants:** Hazira, Vadodara, Kota, **Manuguru** (Telangana), Talcher, Thal, **Tuticorin** – **none at Kalpakkam**.
[99:5:5] - **Anushakti Vidyut Nigam**: NPCIL 51% + **NTPC** 49% (2011).
[99:5:6] - Capacity 8.78 GW → 22.38 GW by 2031-32. Capacity factor: nuclear 85-90%, solar 20-25%, wind 25-35%.
[99:5:7] - **SHANTI Act 2025**: repeals Atomic Energy Act 1962 and **CLNDA 2010**; admits private companies.
[99:5:8] # Fuel and the three-stage programme
[99:5:9] TABLE head: Stage | Reactor / fuel
  [99:5:9.0] I | PHWR – natural uranium + heavy water
  [99:5:9.1] II | Fast breeder – plutonium; breeds more fuel than it uses
  [99:5:9.2] III | Thorium (AHWR)
[99:5:10] - Programme formulated by **Homi J. Bhabha (1954)**, adopted 1958, to use India's thorium.
[99:5:11] - **Uranium:** **Jaduguda, Narwapahar** (Singhbhum, Jharkhand – operated by **UCIL**), **Tummalapalle (YSR Kadapa, AP)**; low-grade ore. World: **Australia** largest reserves (28%), **Kazakhstan** top producer (43%).
[99:5:12] - **Thorium:** monazite and ilmenite beach sands (Kerala, TN, AP, Odisha); about 25% of world's. Budget 2026-27: nil duty on monazite.
[99:5:13] > Exam angle: Tarapur first (1969, USA BWRs), Apsara first reactor, Kudankulam = Russia/TN, Kovvada = USA/AP, Haripur = WB, Kalpakkam no heavy-water plant.
[99:5:14] ~~ Sources: PMF Science & Technology 04 p.61, 67-68 (Apsara 1956, three-stage 1954/1958, Tarapur 1969 BWRs, reactor table, Kovvada AP1000 USA, Jaitapur France); PMF Human & Economic Geography 04 p.51-55 (uranium reserves and production, Jaduguda, Tummalapalle, Kudankulam VVER, heavy-water plants Thal, Manuguru); Rocket Indian Geography p.126 (nuclear plants and States); web search summary (Wikipedia "Obninsk Nuclear Power Plant", Britannica: EBR-I 1951, Obninsk 1954, Calder Hall 1956); app notes 4:99 (sub 6)
## SUB 6 | Hydel Power
[99:6:0] - Most hydel potential lies in the **Himalayan basins** (Brahmaputra, Indus, Ganga): snow-fed, perennial, steep slopes; Peninsular rivers are seasonal. **Arunachal Pradesh** = highest potential.
[99:6:1] - UP-Bihar statement: the Himalaya has huge hydel potential; the Himalaya north of eastern UP and Bihar lies in **Nepal**, so these States depend on treaty projects (Kosi, Gandak).
[99:6:2] - **First hydropower station: Sidrapong, Darjeeling (1897)**; **Shivasamudram** (Kaveri, **Karnataka**, 1902) = second oldest and the oldest among Pykara, Pallivasal, Machkund, Mayurakshi.
[99:6:3] - India ranks **3rd** in the world in number of large dams (after China, USA).
[99:6:4] - **NHPC** (hydro generation PSU) set up 1975. **Pumped storage** stores daytime solar power for night peaks; off-stream closed-loop PSPs no longer need CEA concurrence.
[99:6:5] TABLE head: Project | Location
  [99:6:5.0] Bhakra | Sutlej, HP
  [99:6:5.1] Baira Siul | Siul (Ravi tributary), Chamba, HP
  [99:6:5.2] Salal | Chenab, Reasi, J&K
  [99:6:5.3] Ramganga | Pauri Garhwal, Uttarakhand (multipurpose)
  [99:6:5.4] Tapovan Vishnugad | Chamoli, Uttarakhand
  [99:6:5.5] Tawa / Baglihar / Dulhasti | Narmadapuram MP / Ramban, Kishtwar J&K
  [99:6:5.6] Idukki, Sabarigiri | Kerala – hydel (Idukki is not thermal)
  [99:6:5.7] Ghatprabha | Karnataka – irrigation
  [99:6:5.8] Singrauli small hydro | Sonbhadra, UP (2 x 4 MW)
[99:6:6] - **Mangdechhu** (Bhutan) inaugurated by PM Modi in **2019** (Chhukha, Kurichhu, Dagachhu are older).
[99:6:7] TABLE head: World hydro | Country, river | MW
  [99:6:7.0] Three Gorges | China, Yangtze (Hubei) | 22,500
  [99:6:7.1] Baihetan | China, Jinsha | 16,000
  [99:6:7.2] Itaipu | Brazil-Paraguay, Parana | 14,000
  [99:6:7.3] Xiluodu | China, Jinsha (not Yangtze) | 13,860
  [99:6:7.4] Belo Monte | Brazil, Xingu | 11,200
[99:6:8] > Exam angle: Sidrapong first (1897), Shivasamudram oldest among southern options; project-State match; Mangdechhu 2019.
[99:6:9] ~~ Sources: Rocket Indian Geography p.16 (first hydropower station Sidrapong), p.15 (Shivasamudram, Cauvery, Karnataka), p.64 (Ramganga, Tawa, Baglihar); app notes 4:99 (sub 5); dams rank, UP-Bihar statement: general knowledge, not checked
## SUB 7 | Fuel Gases – Composition and Fuel Properties
[99:7:0] TABLE head: Fuel | Composition
  [99:7:0.0] Natural gas / CNG | Mainly methane (CH4), 70-90%, + ethane
  [99:7:0.1] LPG | Propane + butane
  [99:7:0.2] Biogas / gobar gas | Methane 50-75% + CO2 25-50%; anaerobic digestion; gives power + manure
  [99:7:0.3] Water gas (= syngas) | CO + H2 (steam on coke or hydrocarbons)
  [99:7:0.4] Producer gas | CO + N2 (+ H2)
  [99:7:0.5] Coal gas | H2, CH4, CO
  [99:7:0.6] Petrol / Kerosene / Diesel | C5-C10 / C10-C16 / C15-C18 (fractional distillation of crude)
  [99:7:0.7] Hydrogen | Electrolysis or steam reforming
[99:7:1] - Fractional distillation: lighter fractions boil off first – **gasoline (petrol)** at the lowest temperature, then kerosene, diesel, heavy oil.
[99:7:2] - Calorific value (kJ/kg): hydrogen 1,50,000 > LPG 55,000 > **CNG/methane 50,000** > petrol, diesel 45,000 > biogas > coal (brown coal lower) > wood > dung.
[99:7:3] - CO2 per kWh of heat: **anthracite** (about 0.37 kg) > house coal (0.35) > LPG (about 0.23); wood logs counted nearly carbon-neutral.
[99:7:4] - **Kerosene floats** on water because its **density is less than water's** (and it does not mix).
[99:7:5] - **Alessandro Volta** first isolated methane (marsh gas, 1776) and exploded it with an electric spark.
[99:7:6] - **Green hydrogen** = RE-powered electrolysis; **blue hydrogen** = gas + CCS.
[99:7:7] > Exam angle: match fuel gas with constituents (UPSC 1999, 2004); syngas = water gas = CO + H2; LPG = propane + butane.
[99:7:8] ~~ Sources: Rocket Chemistry p.33 (fuel composition, producer gas, water gas, biogas), p.34 (calorific values); web search summary (SEAI DEAP guide, deapguide.seai.ie: anthracite 0.366, house coal 0.354 kg CO2/kWh); app notes 4:99 (sub 0); coal gas, Volta: general knowledge, not checked
## SUB 8 | Andhra Pradesh Energy
[99:8:0] - **Singareni Collieries (SCCL)**: under the **AP Reorganisation Act 2014 (Twelfth Schedule)** equity = **51% Telangana, 49% Government of India**; AP's share = **nil**. After bifurcation AP **lost its coal mines** – higher coal transport cost.
[99:8:1] - **KG basin**: abundant **natural gas** (KG-D6, Rawa crude); gas feeds **Nagarjuna Fertilizers** (Kakinada).
[99:8:2] - **Tatipaka** (Konaseema) = ONGC mini refinery; **Visakhapatnam** = HPCL refinery and SPR.
[99:8:3] - **Tummalapalle** (Cuddapah basin, **YSR Kadapa**): one of the world's largest uranium reserves (about 1.5 lakh t) – AP's largest uranium district = **Kadapa**.
[99:8:4] - **Kovvada** (Srikakulam) = proposed **atomic (nuclear) power plant** (USA), not a coal mine.
[99:8:5] TABLE head: Station | Location | Capacity
  [99:8:5.0] Simhadri STPS (NTPC) | Visakhapatnam (Anakapalli); seawater | 2,000 MW
  [99:8:5.1] Dr Narla Tata Rao TPS | Ibrahimpatnam, near Vijayawada, NTR (Krishna till 2022) | 2,560 MW; largest AP thermal (Apr 2025)
  [99:8:5.2] Sri Damodaram Sanjeevaiah TPS | Krishnapatnam, Nellore | 2,400 MW
  [99:8:5.3] Rayalaseema TPS | Kalamalla, YSR Kadapa | 1,050 MW
  [99:8:5.4] Srisailam Right Bank | Sunnipenta; Krishna, AP-Telangana border; begun 1968 | 770 MW
  [99:8:5.5] Upper / Lower Sileru | Lower at Mothugudem | Upper Sileru PSP 1,350 MW
  [99:8:5.6] Polavaram hydro | Godavari | 960 MW (12 Kaplan x 80)
  [99:8:5.7] Pinnapuram PSP (Greenko) | Kurnool | 1,680 MW; world's first integrated RE storage
  [99:8:5.8] Kurnool Ultra Mega Solar Park | Panyam, Kurnool | 1,000 MW
  [99:8:5.9] NP Kunta (Ananthapuramu UMSP) | Sri Sathya Sai | -
  [99:8:5.10] ReNew integrated | Ananthapuram | 2.8 GW
[99:8:6] - CEA plan (10 Aug 2026): 7,370 MW PSP + **3,931 MW battery** by 2035-36. AP RE (Dec 2025): 10,639.75 MW. Green Energy Corridor Phase-I finished Dec 2025 (KfW).
[99:8:7] > Exam angle: SCCL 51:49 (AP nil), KG gas, Tummalapalle uranium (Kadapa), Kovvada nuclear.

  [99:9:0.0] Bhadla | Jodhpur, Rajasthan – 2,245 MW, world's largest solar park

[99:9:4] - **Green Energy Corridor**: dedicated transmission to carry RE power to the grid.

[99:9:8] ~~ Sources: app notes 4:99 (sub 1, sub 7, sub 9)

[99:9:10] - **Satara (Maharashtra):** known for **wind farms** (Chalkewadi, Vankusawade); Koyna hydro project is also in Satara district.
[99:9:11] - **Solar leaders:** **Karnataka** had the most installed solar till 2021 (7.4 GW, Dec 2020; Pavagada); **Rajasthan** is 1st since Sept 2021.
[99:9:12] - **Solar potential (NISE/MNRE):** India about 749 GW; **Rajasthan highest (about 142 GW)**, J&K next.

  After the closure of the Strait of Hormuz in March 2026, India responded to energy-supply stress by:
  Relying only on imported crude oil from the same route | Using only hydropower and nuclear power | Stopping all thermal power generation | *Falling back on coal and gas and importing LNG and LPG from other suppliers
  x: The disruption led India to fall back on coal and gas and bring LNG and LPG from alternative suppliers.

  x: India imports about 88% of its crude oil; the Strait of Hormuz closure (Mar 2026) exposed this dependence despite renewable growth. 50% and 70% understate it, and 98% overstates it.

  Which of the following pairs of India's nuclear-programme stage and its basis is correctly matched?
  Stage I – Enriched uranium in light-water reactors | Stage III – Enriched uranium in light-water reactors | *Stage III – Thorium-based AHWR | Stage I – Thorium-based AHWR
  x: Stage III is thorium-based (AHWR); Stage I uses PHWRs fuelled by natural uranium with heavy water as moderator.
[nf3995aeaed0] sub5
  At what capacity factor do nuclear power plants generally operate?
  20–25% | 25–35% | 60–70% | *85–90%
  x: Nuclear plants generally run at 85–90%, against 20–25% for solar and 25–35% for wind.
[n0d11edba007] sub5

  x: Tummalapalle uranium is in YSR Kadapa district, AP. Jaduguda and Narwapahar are the Jharkhand (Singhbhum) mines; Srikakulam is the Kovvada nuclear-plant site, not a uranium deposit; Nellore is not cited for uranium. Indian uranium ore is low-grade.

  x: Jaitapur is planned with France (six EPRs); Kudankulam is with Russia, Kovvada with the USA and Tarapur has US reactors.

  x: Stage II uses fast breeders fuelled by plutonium, which breed more fuel than they use.

=== NOTES 4:100 | Patterns and Issues in Transport and Trade - Regional Imbalance, Connectivity, Logistics
## SUB 0 | Transcontinental and Famous Railways of the World
[100:0:0] # Trans-Siberian Railway (asked 6 times)
[100:0:1] - **Longest continuous / longest continental railway in the world** (SSC CGL 2015) – not the Canadian National, Canadian Pacific or a "Transatlantic Railway".
[100:0:2] - **Main line: Moscow (Yaroslavsky station) → Vladivostok**, **9,289 km**, crosses **8 time zones** (APPSC G1 Prelims 2024 → **Moscow to Vladivostok**).
[100:0:3]   - NCERT XII / PMF describe the route from **St. Petersburg (Leningrad)** in the west to **Vladivostok** (9,332 km) – the classic "Leningrad to Vladivostok" pair in match questions.
[100:0:4] - **Built mainly 1891–1916** (work began 1891; through route 1904–1916); opened Siberia to European markets; crosses the **Urals**, the **Ob and Yenisei**.
[100:0:5] - **Baikal–Amur Mainline (BAM):** runs **parallel** to the Trans-Siberian – the alternative strategic route.
[100:0:6] - **On the route (west → east):** St. Petersburg, Moscow, Yaroslavl, Kirov, Perm, Yekaterinburg, Tyumen, **Omsk**, Novosibirsk, Krasnoyarsk, Irkutsk (fur centre), Ulan-Ude, **Chita** (agro-centre), **Khabarovsk**, **Vladivostok**; **Kazan** lies on a connecting branch that joins near Yekaterinburg and is counted on the route in exam keys.
[100:0:7] - **NOT on it:** **Sochi** (Black Sea resort, UPPCS 2019), **Murmansk** (far north-west), **Georgia** (a separate country, APPSC G1 2003).
[100:0:8] - **Vladivostok:** Pacific terminus, HQ of Russia's Pacific Fleet; permanent venue of the **Eastern Economic Forum** (since 2015).
[100:0:9] # Other transcontinental and famous lines
[100:0:10] TABLE head: Railway | Route | Fact
  [100:0:10.0] Canadian Pacific | Montreal – Vancouver | Canada's first transcontinental, completed 1885
  [100:0:10.1] Trans-Canadian (NCERT) | Halifax – Vancouver, 7,050 km, via Montreal, Ottawa, Winnipeg, Calgary | "Economic artery of Canada"; wheat and meat
  [100:0:10.2] Canadian National | Atlantic to Pacific | Major freight network
  [100:0:10.3] US Transcontinental (Union Pacific + Central Pacific) | Union Pacific from Omaha, Central Pacific from Sacramento; met at Promontory Summit, Utah, 10 May 1869 (Golden Spike) | North America's first transcontinental; Union and Pacific line New York – San Francisco
  [100:0:10.4] Orient Express | Paris – Istanbul via Strasbourg, Munich, Vienna, Budapest, Bucharest/Belgrade | Famous luxury train; crosses France, Germany, Austria, Hungary, Romania
  [100:0:10.5] Trans-Andean | Buenos Aires (Argentina) – Valparaiso (Chile), Uspallata Pass 3,900 m | South America's only transcontinental; crosses the Andes – not the highest railway
  [100:0:10.6] European Trans-Continental | Paris – Warsaw | Match-question pair
  [100:0:10.7] Qinghai–Tibet | Xining – Lhasa | Highest railway in the world
  [100:0:10.8] Trans-Australian | Port Augusta – Kalgoorlie (1917); Perth – Sydney line via Kalgoorlie, Broken Hill | Nullarbor Plain: world's longest straight track, 478 km; the Indian Pacific runs Sydney – Perth
  [100:0:10.9] Channel Tunnel (Eurotunnel) | England (UK) – France under the English Channel | London – Paris rail link (RAS 1997)
[100:0:11] - **Trans-Canada Highway (road):** **Vancouver (west) – St. John's, Newfoundland (east)**; exam option "Saint John City to Vancouver" (BPSC 2018). Alaskan Highway: Edmonton – Anchorage.
[100:0:12] > Match (IAS 1995, BPSC 2005): European Trans-Continental – Paris–Warsaw; Trans-Andean – Buenos Aires–Valparaiso; Trans-Siberian – Leningrad–Vladivostok; Orient Express – Paris–Istanbul → **5 4 2 1**.
[100:0:13] > Trap: Canadian Pacific = **Montreal – Vancouver**; Halifax – Vancouver is the NCERT "Trans-Canadian" line. Longest = Trans-Siberian; highest = Qinghai–Tibet.
[100:0:14] ~~ Sources: NextGen Rocket World Geography #17 p.27-28, #6 p.7; OnlyIAS NCERT Human Geography Part 8 p.14-19 (book pp.91-95); PMF Human & Economic Geography Part 2 p.5-10 (book pp.118-122); en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-Siberian_Railway (infobox); app notes 4:100 sub 8
## SUB 1 | World Railway Length, Rank and High-speed Rail
[100:1:0] # Longest networks
[100:1:1] TABLE head: Rank | Country | Length (approx.)
  [100:1:1.0] 1 | USA | 220,000–250,000 km (mostly freight)
  [100:1:1.1] 2 | China | ~159,000–165,000 km; largest high-speed network (~48,000 km, end 2024)
  [100:1:1.2] 3 | Russia | ~105,000 km
  [100:1:1.3] 4 | India | ~69,439 route km (March 2025)
  [100:1:1.4] 5 | Canada | ~49,000 km
  [100:1:1.5] – | Germany | ~33,000–39,800 km
[100:1:2] - **India: 4th largest network** in route length (APPSC FSO flashcard) and the **2nd largest under a single management**.
[100:1:3] - **Descending order (UPPSC 2014 key): USA, Canada, India, Germany** – older data; **now USA > India > Canada > Germany**.
[100:1:4] - **Longest high-speed / bullet-train network: China** (UKPSC 2016) – not Japan, Russia or the USA.
[100:1:5] # Density
[100:1:6] - Among **Canada, USA, Russia and India**, **India** has the densest railway network.
[100:1:7] - Densest national networks: small industrial European countries (Switzerland, Czech Republic, Belgium); Europe has one of the densest rail networks.
[100:1:8] - **North America** has the highest road density and most vehicles; railways there carry bulky freight more than passengers.
[100:1:9] ~~ Sources: NextGen Rocket World Geography #17 p.27-28; PMF Human & Economic Geography Part 2 p.7 (book p.120); en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_rail_transport_network_size; english.www.gov.cn and China Daily (48,000 km HSR, end 2024); app notes 4:100 sub 7
## SUB 2 | Regional Imbalance – Road and Rail Density in India
[100:2:0] TABLE head: Density | Highest | Lowest
  [100:2:0.0] Rail | Punjab, Haryana, Ganga plains | North-East, Himalaya, Thar (Rajasthan desert)
  [100:2:0.1] Road | Kerala | Arunachal Pradesh (recent MoRTH data); J&K 12.14 km per 100 sq km (2011, NCERT)
[100:2:1] - **Highest road density: Kerala** (Jharkhand PCS 2021) – **6,690.30 km per 1,000 sq km** (31 March 2019); NCERT: 517.77 km per 100 sq km, national average 142.68 (2011).
[100:2:2] - Road density order among the PYQ's States (2019, km per 1,000 sq km): **Kerala 6,690.30 > Punjab 2,935.98 > Gujarat 1,270.73 > Haryana 1,137.53**.
[100:2:3] - **Road density** = length of road per 100 (or 1,000) sq km of area.
[100:2:4] # AP NH density vs India
[100:2:5] TABLE head: Measure | AP | India
  [100:2:5.0] Per lakh population | 17.64 km | 12.09 km
  [100:2:5.1] Per 1,000 sq km | 53.65 km | 44.46 km
[100:2:6] - AP is **above** the India average on **both** NH density measures.
[100:2:7] ~~ Sources: OnlyIAS NCERT Human Geography Part 8 p.13 (book p.89); PMF Human & Economic Geography Part 2 p.24-25 (book p.137); PYQ data (MoRTH, 31 March 2019); app notes 4:100 sub 0
## SUB 3 | Highways, Corridors and Rural Roads
[100:3:0] # Corridors
[100:3:1] TABLE head: Corridor | End points
  [100:3:1.0] East-West Corridor | Silchar (Assam) – Porbandar (Gujarat), ~3,300–3,640 km
  [100:3:1.1] North-South Corridor | Srinagar – Kanyakumari (with Kochi–Salem spur), ~4,000 km
  [100:3:1.2] Golden Quadrilateral | Delhi – Kolkata – Chennai – Mumbai (5,846 km); longest stretch in AP
[100:3:2] - E-W and N-S Corridors **meet at Jhansi** (UP).
[100:3:3] - **East-West Corridor is not Dibrugarh – Surat** (UPSC 2023).
[100:3:4] - **Bharatmala Pariyojana:** Phase I approved October **2017**, **34,800 km** of NH corridors; freight and passenger efficiency (APPSC 2019).
[100:3:5] - **NH 44** (Srinagar – Kanyakumari) = longest NH.
[100:3:6] # Rural roads – PMGSY
[100:3:7] - **PMGSY:** launched **25 December 2000**; **fully funded Centrally Sponsored Scheme**; aim: **all-weather road connectivity** to unconnected rural habitations (UPPCS 2001: "road connectivity" option) – not police patrolling or crime response.
[100:3:8] TABLE head: PMGSY eligibility | Population
  [100:3:8.0] Plain areas | 500+
  [100:3:8.1] NE, hill, tribal areas | 250+
  [100:3:8.2] LWE areas | 100+
[100:3:9] - PMGSY has reached about **99% of eligible habitations** (2026).
[100:3:10] - Tribal roads (RCPLWE) in AP: 27 roads + 14 bridges (397 km) in Srikakulam, Vizianagaram, Visakhapatnam, East Godavari.
[100:3:11] # North-East rail gap
[100:3:12] - **Bairabi–Sairang line** (Mizoram): **48 tunnels**; put **Aizawl** on the rail map (**September 2025**) – the **4th** NE capital on rail after Guwahati, Agartala, Itanagar.
[100:3:13] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Geography - APPSC Exams Part 8.1 p.3 (book p.143); OnlyIAS NCERT Human Geography Part 8 p.15 (book p.92); PMF Human & Economic Geography Part 2 p.6, p.25 (book pp.119, 137); PMF Lens 1 Mar 2026 (PMGSY 99%); app notes 4:100 sub 1-2
## SUB 4 | Logistics Programmes and Freight Corridors
[100:4:0] TABLE head: Programme | Key fact
  [100:4:0.0] PM GatiShakti NMP | Launched 13 October 2021; GIS-based integrated planning platform; 16 ministries at launch (EGoS now 23)
  [100:4:0.1] GatiShakti rail corridors | Energy-Mineral-Cement, Port Connectivity, High Traffic Density; 434 projects, Rs 11.17 lakh crore
  [100:4:0.2] Sagarmala | Five pillars: port modernisation, port connectivity, port-led industry, coastal communities, coastal shipping / IWT – not railway electrification
  [100:4:0.3] MMLPs | 35 planned; e.g. Jogighopa (Assam)
  [100:4:0.4] e-FAST India | Electric Freight Accelerator for Sustainable Transport; NITI Aayog + WRI India, September 2022; India's first national electric-freight platform; PACT (Platform for Aggregating Clean Transport) launched at the 5th e-FAST India Summit, 2026

  [100:4:2.0] Eastern DFC | Ludhiana – Sonnagar | 1,337 km, World Bank
  [100:4:2.1] Western DFC | Dadri – JNPT | 1,506 km, JICA
[100:4:3] - Full DFC network (2,843 km) operational; last Western DFC stretches opened 10 Sep 2026.
[100:4:4] - **Sonnagar – Dankuni PPP** = a **failed** project.
[100:4:5] > Trap: Eastern DFC = World Bank; Western DFC = JICA. e-FAST is "Freight Accelerator for Sustainable Transport" – not "Fast", "Accumulator" or "Simulated".

## SUB 5 | Logistics Cost, Policy and Indices
[100:5:0] - **Logistics cost:** **7.97% of GDP (2023-24)** official estimate – debated against the older **13–14%** figure.
[100:5:1] - **National Logistics Policy 2022:** single-digit logistics cost; **top 25 in the LPI by 2030**.
[100:5:2] - **World Bank LPI 2023:** India **38th**.
[100:5:3] - **LEADS = Logistics Ease Across Different States:** by **DPIIT**, began **2018**; modelled on the World Bank LPI; mixes **perception and objective data**; ranks **Indian States/UTs** (the LPI ranks countries).
[100:5:4] TABLE head: LEADS 2025 group | Top performer
  [100:5:4.0] Coastal States | Tamil Nadu
  [100:5:4.1] Landlocked States | Uttar Pradesh
  [100:5:4.2] North-Eastern States | Mizoram
  [100:5:4.3] Union Territories | Delhi
[100:5:5] - AP: **"Achiever"** among coastal States in **LEADS 2023**; **Accelerator** in LEADS 2025 (new tiers: Exemplars, High Performers, Accelerators, Growth Seekers).
[100:5:6] ~~ Sources: app notes 4:100 sub 4
## SUB 6 | AP – Coastline, Port Hinterland and Rail Links
[100:6:0] - **AP coastline: 1,053.07 km – 3rd longest** after Gujarat (2,340.62 km) and Tamil Nadu (1,068.69 km) in the revised count (India 11,098.81 km; PIB 4 Dec 2025). Old figure **974 km, "2nd"** (APPSC AMVI 2023 key) is outdated.
[100:6:1] - Rail carries cement, granite, minerals to **Visakhapatnam, Gangavaram, Krishnapatnam**.
[100:6:2] - **Kottavalasa–Kirandul (KK) line:** carries **Bailadila iron ore** to Visakhapatnam port through **Eastern Ghats tunnels**.
[100:6:3] - **Guntakal–Ballari 3rd and 4th lines:** link Rayalaseema to **Karnataka and Goa**, plus Krishnapatnam and Ramayapatnam ports; Rs 1,264 crore; target 2028-29 (CCEA, July 2026).
[100:6:4] - Gap: last-mile rail to industrial nodes **Kopparthy and Orvakal** incomplete – industry depends on road.
[100:6:5] - **South Coast Railway:** **18th zone**; HQ **Visakhapatnam**; operational 1 June 2026; under the **AP Reorganisation Act 2014**.
[100:6:6] ~~ Sources: NextGen Rocket AP Geography p.26; RUNBOOK settled facts (PIB Release 2198800, 4 Dec 2025); app notes 4:100 sub 3
## SUB 7 | Trade Concentration by Port and State
[100:7:0] - Sea routes carry ~**95%** of India's trade by volume.
[100:7:1] - FY 2026 cargo: **Deendayal (Kandla) 160.11 MT** > Paradip > JNPA.
[100:7:2] - Non-major leader: **Krishnapatnam** (41.09 MMT, Apr–Nov 2025).
[100:7:3] - ~**75% of transhipment cargo** goes via **Colombo or Singapore**.
[100:7:4] - AP: **6th largest exporting State**; **1st in tobacco** and sea-food exports.
[100:7:5] - Historic: **Silk Route** = land trade route; **Spice Routes** = sea routes.
[100:7:6] ~~ Sources: app notes 4:100 sub 5

[100:8:0] TABLE head: Factor | Fact
  [100:8:0.0] Rail vs road | Rail freight ~90% less CO2, costs about half
  [100:8:0.1] Rail share of freight | ~27%; National Rail Plan target 45% by 2030
  [100:8:0.2] Waterways | Budget 2026-27: 20 new National Waterways in five years, starting with NW-5 (Odisha)
